\subsection{Path B: Literature-Calibrated Microsimulation}\label{sec:pathb}

\subsubsection{Specification}

The first hazard path brings competing risks, literature-calibrated elasticities, and a cleaner isolation of the Danish counterfactual, without reintroducing the utility-maximizing machinery of the ABM. It simulates a stratified sample of 75,000 loans, 40,234 of which are still active when the QT window opens (Appendix~\ref{app:params}). Each loan carries static covariates---FICO, region, original LTV, coupon, and a stratum identifier keyed on the Path A specification less its LTV axis---and dynamic state: loan age, balance, and a regime-specific rate gap. Equity enters only through the static original-LTV covariate: no updated CLTV and no local house-price feed exists anywhere in the pipeline, so equity dynamics over the window are unmodeled. That is a disclosed design boundary for this turnover-dominated, deeply out-of-the-money window; the FICO/LTV covariate ablation reported below brackets what the static block contributes. I specify each loan's monthly hazard of prepayment as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pathB}
h^{\mathrm{prep}}_{i,t} = \max\!\left\{\, \underline{h},\; h_0\!\left(a_{i,t}\right) \exp\!\left( \beta_1\, g_{i,t} + \beta_F\, z^{\mathrm{FICO}}_{i} + \beta_L\, z^{\mathrm{LTV}}_{i} + \beta_B\, B_{s(i),t} \right) \right\},
\end{equation}
with a competing hazard of default
\begin{equation}\label{eq:default}
h^{\mathrm{def}}_{i,t} = h^{\mathrm{def}}_0 \exp\!\big( \gamma_r\, \sigma_{i,t} + \gamma_F\, z^{\mathrm{FICO}}_{i} + \gamma_L\, z^{\mathrm{LTV}}_{i} \big), \qquad \sigma_{i,t} = \max\{0,\ -\tilde g_{i,t}\},
\end{equation}
where $\sigma_{i,t}$ is rate stress ($\tilde g_{i,t} = g_{i,t}/100$ is the rate gap of \eqref{eq:pathB} expressed in decimal units, so $\sigma$ is the decimal magnitude of an out-of-the-money gap), and $h^{\mathrm{def}}_0 = 3\times10^{-4}$ monthly, a prior implying $0.36\%$ per year against a realized Freddie Mac default rate of $0.31\%$ over the window on this paper's own GMAR extraction. The calibration did not use that corroboration: the prior was fixed before that series was assembled. Every Path B parameter is fixed a priori in the configuration, and none is estimated on the benchmark; Appendix~\ref{app:params} collects the full parameter vector with provenance, together with the delinquency transition matrix. Table~\ref{tab:hazard-notation} maps every symbol here to its Path A counterpart. Here $g_{i,t}$ is the rate gap: the loan's coupon minus the prevailing market rate, in 100 bp units, negative when the borrower is locked in. The positive $\beta_1$ of \eqref{eq:beta1} therefore suppresses prepayment exactly when the par-payoff penalty binds. $B_{s(i),t} \in [0,1]$ is the cohort-level burnout state of loan $i$'s stratum $s(i)$: the stratum's cumulative prepaid UPB as a share of its original UPB, capped at one. Every stratum is initialised at zero at the window open, so the state accumulates only within-window prepayment against an original-UPB denominator that still includes pre-window-terminated loans---window-cumulative, not life-cumulative. With $\beta_B = -0.5$, the burnout state scales the hazard by $e^{-0.5 B} \in [0.61, 1]$. Finally, $\underline{h}$ is the baseline turnover floor: the monthly single-month-mortality (SMM) equivalent of 4\% annual CPR, $\underline{h} = 1-(1-0.04)^{1/12} \approx 0.0034$. It floors total turnover, not a strictly involuntary component of it: deep-discount-cohort turnover at this level includes discretionary life-cycle moves and cash-out refinancings alongside strictly involuntary events. It is also held \emph{flat in loan age}, an assumption the very ladder used to calibrate it does not support. Pooled 2017--2019 out-of-the-money turnover rises monotonically with age, from 3.68\% below twelve months to 10.99\% at twenty-four to thirty-six (Appendix~\ref{app:floormech}). I flag the assumption; I do not sweep it. The reason is the same appendix's second finding: the 10.99\% cell is carried entirely by the 2019 falling-rate leg, so the age profile is substantially refinancing-contaminated, and an age-varying floor built from it would inherit that contamination. An age dimension therefore remains the one calibration axis this paper sweeps in neither level nor time, and a clean version of it needs a rate-regime-pure age ladder this design does not have. No component decomposition exists in this design either, and the strictly-involuntary share (death, divorce, and forced relocation) is plausibly well under half. The floor's semantics do institutional work in selecting the U.S.-intercept Danish anchor (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}) and in retaining the max floor form (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). There the operative premise is only that baseline turnover cannot fall below its observed deep-discount level when the payoff rule changes, which the total-turnover reading supports. Those choices are conventions consistent with that premise, not consequences of a strictly-involuntary decomposition. The floor's level is anchored to the same empirical observation as the ABM's mobility calibration (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}): turnover on deeply out-of-the-money discount cohorts during 2023--2024, which lies \emph{inside} the June 2022--November 2025 evaluation window. No Path B parameter touches the benchmark dollar figure, but the specification's dominant level-setter is measured from in-window prepayment behavior. The verification content below is the marginal over the $\beta_1 = 0$ null, not the aggregate level itself. The pre-committed floor sweep of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} shows that even this marginal is conditional on the floor calibration: it spans $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ points at the central elasticity across the empirically plausible 3--5\% floor range ($+9.2$ at the production 4\% floor). That interval, not any point inside it, is the identified object. Its positivity at every floor and elasticity-band point swept is a consistency check the window's rate configuration cannot let fail (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). The floor's functional form matters too. Replacing the hard maximum of \eqref{eq:pathB} with a competing-risks combination, under which the elasticity is never censored, moves the production marginal to $+11.25$ points and makes it nearly floor-invariant ($+11.29$ to $+11.21$ across the same 3--5\% range; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The max-form marginal's floor-dependence is censoring-driven. It is not evidence about the elasticity. The floor binds in 36\% of the 1,683,124 evaluated loan-months in the production U.S. run. A substantial share of the aggregate level rides on the floor, consistent with the no-lock-in null of Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}, which shows the rate-inelastic model recovers 97.8\% of the benchmark under the standalone scorer (88.7\% on the shared accounting basis). Those are production-floor-form levels: under the additive form the same null recovers 44.7\% at the off-window floor against the production form's 94.8\%, both on the standalone scorer (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). Proportionality in \eqref{eq:pathB} accordingly holds above the floor, not through it. The baseline hazard $h_0$ follows the 100~PSA seasoning ramp (CPR rising 0.2 percentage points per month of loan age to 6\% at month 30), applied at monthly frequency via $\mathrm{SMM} = 1-(1-\mathrm{CPR})^{1/12}$. The rate-gap coefficient is calibrated from \citet{liebersohn2024}, who estimate that a 100 bp increase in the prevailing rate above the locked-in coupon reduces quarterly mobility probability by 5.5\% to 7.7\% depending on specification. I adopt the 6.5\% midpoint of that band as the ex-ante central scenario, with the band edges as sensitivity bounds, converted to a monthly hazard via the survival-function transform
\begin{equation}\label{eq:beta1}
\beta_1 = -\ln\!\left[\frac{1-\big(1-(1-\delta)\,P_q\big)^{1/3}}{1-\big(1-P_q\big)^{1/3}}\right],
\end{equation}
where $\delta$ is the proportional quarterly mobility decline per 100 bp of rate gap, $P_q = 0.06$ a reference baseline turnover probability occupying the transform's quarterly slot, and the inner terms are the monthly single-month mortalities implied by constant-hazard compounding within the quarter, $1 - P_q = (1 - h_m)^3$. The reference probability is a conversion auxiliary, not a measured quarterly mobility rate, and it sits well above \citepos{liebersohn2024} own 1.5\% zero-gap quarterly moving level (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}). I retain it because the conversion is nearly insensitive to it over $P_q \in (0, 0.12]$. Evaluated at the central decline, $|\beta_1|$ is $0.0675$, $0.0679$, $0.0686$ and $0.0701$ at $P_q = 0.015$, $0.03$, $0.06$ and $0.12$: quadrupling the assumed moving level above the source's own moving level moves the imported coefficient by under four percent (Appendix~\ref{app:params}). Equation~\eqref{eq:beta1} is the transform implemented in the codebase. At the central $\delta = 0.065$ it evaluates to $\beta_1 = 0.0686$ (unrounded $0.068571$), the value carried in the production configuration and rounded to $0.069$ in Table~\ref{tab:params}. The superseded continuous-hazard variant, a distinct object the simulation does not use, is documented in Appendix~\ref{app:params}. The transform yields the dimensionless log-hazard-ratio implied by a quarterly probability decline (it replaces a divide-by-three approximation, not a hazard itself), and \eqref{eq:pathB} then applies $\beta_1$ linearly per 100 bp of gap. At QT-era gaps of roughly $-380$ bp this compounds the local quarterly estimate to a hazard ratio of about $0.77$ ($\approx e^{3.8 \times -0.069}$), a log-linear extrapolation beyond the range in which the source elasticity was estimated. I flag it as a functional-form assumption and test it directly with a pre-committed concave-transform rerun of the paired legs. The extrapolation proves immaterial to the level and material to the marginal on the relative reading that ranking rests on, and the transform dimension travels with the marginal's operative uncertainty (Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}; the test and its materiality reading are in Appendix~\ref{app:params}).

\subsubsection{Transport assumptions}

Two transport assumptions travel with the import, and I name both here. The first is an aggregation transport that assumes response homogeneity: the source estimand is a ZIP-code-level moving hazard applied as a loan-level log-hazard coefficient. The second transport concerns survival: the estimand is estimated on a population not conditioned on mortgage survival, while \eqref{eq:pathB} applies it to a pool from which 34,734 of the 75,000 sampled loans had already prepaid before the window opened---a survival-selected pool depleted of the rate-responsive. This transport's direction is therefore signed toward attenuation, and priced. Under the gamma-frailty selection identity the attenuation is $\eta = S_{\mathrm{pre}}^{\xi}$ at the measured pre-window survival share $S_{\mathrm{pre}}$. The off-window marginal then reads $+5.1$/$+4.6$/$+3.7$ points at $\xi = 0.25/0.5/1$ (run \texttt{attenuation\_sensitivity}). The gamma-frailty parameter $\xi$ is not estimable in this design and is displayed as the free parameter. Path A's burnout coefficient, the one object that speaks to it, is not distinguishable from zero under either bootstrap scheme.

Each month both hazards are evaluated for every active loan. Appendix~\ref{app:params} holds the production step order (fractional prepayment, the default draw and delinquency pipeline, the proportional clamp that never binds), the competing-risks taxonomy of the construction, and the parameter table (Table~\ref{tab:params}).

The transform prices how the elasticity extrapolates in the gap. A second mapping assumption governs what it applies to: \citepos{liebersohn2024} estimand is a moving probability, while \eqref{eq:pathB} applies the implied $\beta_1$ to the entire voluntary hazard, moving and refinancing alike. A pre-committed mixture bracket prices the convention at both floors (runs \texttt{moving\_share\_bracket}, \texttt{moving\_share\_bracket\_offwindow}): $1-\mu$ of the voluntary hazard at its no-response level, $\mu$ at the full response, turnover floor unscaled, parity at $\mu = 1$. The marginal is $+\$37.7$ billion ($+4.9$ points) at $\mu = 0.5$ and $+\$19.2$ billion ($+2.5$ points) at $\mu = 0.25$, against the production $+\$70.3$ billion at $\mu = 1$, close to proportional in $\mu$. At the headlined 4.991\% off-window floor it is $+\$25.7$ billion ($+3.36$ points) at $\mu = 0.5$ and $+\$13.9$ billion ($+1.82$ points) at $\mu = 0.25$, against $+\$42.6$ billion at $\mu = 1$. That scaling is mildly super-proportional, since the higher floor censors the elasticity in more loan-months and censored contributions do not scale with $\mu$. For this book's rate configuration the convention is therefore signed: applying the imported elasticity to less of the hazard can only shrink the marginal, roughly by its share. No termination-reason field exists in the ingested data \citep[cf.][on the refinancing-versus-moving mix]{amromin2020}, so $\mu$ is a bracketing parameter rather than an estimate, and the production $\mu = 1$ remains the reported form. \citet{graybill2026} corroborate the imported band on exactly this margin: their transaction-record sale-hazard estimate measures the moving component that $\mu$ varies.

The floor enters \eqref{eq:pathB} as a single constant $\underline{h}$ broadcast to every loan-month, with no rate-gap, calendar-time, or covariate argument. Baseline turnover is therefore treated as acyclical, and every rate-responsive movement in prepayment is assigned to the elastic channel. The marginal-over-null design cancels the floor's level: the central and $\beta_1 = 0$ legs carry the identical constant. It does not cancel a floor co-varying with the rate cycle. To the extent that lock-in also suppresses involuntary moves, that co-movement is absorbed into the estimated elastic marginal. The in-window marginal is therefore best read as total rate-attributable prepayment suppression, an upper bound on the strictly-voluntary elasticity's contribution. At the off-window calibration the ordering reverses. The demotion's window component (roughly \$20.9 billion; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}) is baseline turnover that the off-window floor classifies as rate-suppressed and the marginal no longer counts, so the off-window $+\$42.6$ billion bounds total rate-attributable suppression from below; the matched-depth $+\$63.5$ billion restores that component. The two calibrations bracket two estimands. They are not conservative and liberal readings of one estimand. The level sweep there already brackets any plausible constant-floor excursion (the 3--5\% range keeps the marginal within $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ points at the central elasticity). A within-run cyclical variant sets $\underline{h}_t = 4\% \times (1 + \kappa z_t)$, with $z_t$ the window-standardized 30-year mortgage rate and $\kappa = 0$ nesting (and in the run exactly reproducing) the production specification. It moves the marginal between $+7.1$ and $+9.4$ points across $\kappa \in [-0.5, +0.5]$, every cell inside the level sweep's bracket. Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor} decomposes that grid with a pre-committed permutation run. Most of its span survives losing the floor path's time order, so the grid measures floor dispersion before it measures cyclicality. The surviving co-movement component is real, small, and bounded there. The window-mean invariant fails, via the floor's non-negativity clip, in the stress cell that sets the bracket's low end.

\subsubsection{Aggregation and the bounds that travel with it}

Aggregation scales simulated prepaid UPB by the ratio of the Fed's MBS book to the 75,000-loan sample balance, with no reweighting toward Ginnie Mae collateral (20.4\% of book face) or vintages outside 2017--2021. The Freddie-estimated hazards extrapolate to those segments as-is. This is not behaviorally one-to-one. Ginnie Mae pools are FHA/VA collateral with a distinct borrower composition and a servicer buyout channel absent from the conventional book, one whose exercise turns on servicer balance-sheet capacity as well as borrower behaviour \citep{aiello2022}. The framework therefore bounds the composition error of treating the Ginnie share as conventional. It does not model that error. Along the coupon dimension, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}'s full-book reweighting (sample WAC 3.9\% mapped to the book's 2.49\%) moves Path B from 107.0\% to 109.1\%, about two percentage points. That is a mixed-basis figure: 3.9\% is an unweighted mean of borrower note rates, 2.49\% a face-weighted mean of security pass-through coupons. Converted to one convention, the move is $+0.2$ points (run \texttt{coupon\_convention\_reweight}). Along the agency dimension, Ginnie Mae fixed-rate speeds ran 1.2--2.8 percentage points above Freddie Mac's across the window (5.2\% versus 4.0\%, February 2023; 10.3\% versus 7.5\%, May 2025; Ginnie Mae Global Markets Analysis Report, Recursion data). The involuntary buyout channel is a large minority of that difference, not most of it. The forty-two-month mean Ginnie--Freddie CPR gap of $+2.14$ points splits into a voluntary CRR (conditional repayment rate, prepayment net of the default-driven channel) $+1.35$ (63\%) and a buyout CDR $+0.85$ (40\%). The shares sum past one hundred because the CPR $\approx$ CRR $+$ CDR extraction carries a small residual. The most buyout-favourable snapshot (CDR 2.1\% versus 0.4\%, May 2025) is the buyout-dominated month, not the representative one. Treating the 20.4\% Ginnie face share as conventional thus mis-states book-wide CPR by at most $0.204 \times (1.2\text{--}2.8) \approx 0.24$--$0.57$ percentage points. At the null-to-central sensitivity of roughly \$83 billion per CPR point (window-average book face times one point of annual prepayment times three and a half years), that gives a bound of \$20--\$47 billion (2.6--6.2\% of benchmark), signed toward overstating trapped liquidity. The bound is one-sided because the observed differential is: published speeds run Ginnie above Freddie throughout, so the Ginnie-slower branch, signed toward understating trapped liquidity, does not arise in this data.

The vintage dimension carries no bound of either kind. The 2022 vintage alone is 23.1\% of book face, and pre-2017 vintages another 10.6\% (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-wal}), both outside the 2017--2021 Freddie sample. The 2022 vintage's lock-in state is not qualitatively different from the sampled vintages', though: 72.2\% of its face carries a pass-through coupon below 3.0\% (SOMA CUSIP joint coupon $\times$ vintage cells, run \texttt{composition\_shift}), predominantly late-2021/H1-2022 production four to five points below the window's market rates, with only 13.2\% at 4.0\% or above. What the extrapolation lacks is a different \emph{seasoning} state, and neither bound above constrains that. An observed-speed bound in the same template does: per-quarter cohort cells from the Fannie Mae replication ingest carry observed QT-window prepayment for the out-of-sample vintages. Over the window the 2022 vintage prepaid at 4.48\% against the sampled universe's 4.30\% ($+0.17$ unrounded), and the pre-2017 vintages at 5.26\% ($+0.96$; the 2015--2016 cohorts are 99.99\% of that tail's exposure and reproduce its speed unchanged). The share-weighted book-CPR error is $+0.14$ points, or \$11.7 billion at the same \$83-billion-per-CPR-point sensitivity. That is 1.5\% of benchmark, below the static Ginnie bound's own lower edge, and more than a quarter of the headline lock-in marginal (27.6\% of the off-window \$42.6 billion; a sixth, 16.7\%, of the in-sample \$70.3 billion), signed the same conservative direction. Out-of-sample vintages prepaid \emph{faster}, so trapped liquidity is if anything overstated. Applied as a marginal overlay, the template confines the identified marginal to the in-window-vintage share: $+3.7$ points. The overlay scores the 33.7\% out-of-window vintage share by observed Fannie speeds in \emph{both} legs, so that share contributes zero marginal. The result is pure share scaling ($0.66\times$ against a nominal retained share of $0.663$, placebo within $0.0002$), the vintage counterpart of the Ginnie overlay's $+4.4$. The two overlays cannot be composed without the agency-by-vintage cross-tabulation, since 2.4\% of book face is the 2022--Ginnie intersection. Three caveats travel with the bound: Fannie observed speeds proxy the book's; the pre-2017 leg is a seasoned acquisition tail, not a random draw of old originations; and the 2022 leg is a full-year speed applied to an H1-tilted face.

Burnout is deliberately cohort-level; Appendix~\ref{app:params} gives the construction and why a per-loan fractional-burnout measure is not well-defined in this design.

I extend the static Ginnie bound to a time-varying overlay built from the published speed series; Appendix~\ref{app:params} gives its construction, and its book-wide image enters the assembly of Table~\ref{tab:assembly}.

This framework's pipeline carries two Danish counterfactual mechanisms, and only one is live in production; Appendix~\ref{app:params} specifies both and the anchor that selects between them (results in Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}).

\subsubsection{Simulated recovery and the calibration band}

Run on the 40,234 window-start survivors of the 75,000-loan stratified sample from the same Freddie Mac 2017--2021 origination universe (attrition accounting in Appendix~\ref{app:params}), this specification recovers \$818.5 billion, or 107.0\%, of the empirical benchmark. Raw CPR correlation against the empirical path is $r = +0.190$ at zero lag, and peak cross-correlation $r = +0.404$ at lag $-3$. Both are descriptors of the published series, not credentials: four clip-induced zero months dominate the monthly benchmark's differenced signal, so it cannot adjudicate timing (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor}). (A CUSIP-level factor-paydown series would remove those artifacts at the source and is the institutionally superior path for future work; the benchmark retains current-face differencing because factor data were not staged in this pipeline.) The cross-correlation at lag $k$ pairs the simulated series at month $t$ with the empirical series at month $t+k$, both demeaned over the overlap and with boundary months dropped. A direction check on synthetic data confirms that a peak at $k=-3$ means the simulated path \emph{trails} the empirical path by three months. The definition was inverted in print, but the direction claims were always computed under the implemented convention (the replication package's ledger). The opposite reading survives in \texttt{extension\_risk\_results.json}'s \texttt{lag\_interpretation} field (a negative lag means the hazard path \emph{leads}); that string is superseded and retired, left unedited because the artifact is a sha-pinned replication object. At this peak the empirical SOMA-derived CPR path moves approximately three months ahead of the simulated path---a peak selected across the seven examined lags, so its nominal significance is overstated, and no inference rests on this single coefficient. The level and band follow the rate-gap coefficient's unit convention of Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline}; the superseded figure is catalogued, with provenance, in the replication package's ledger.

Importing Path B's elasticity unmodified has two consequences. First, the rate-gap elasticity imported from \citet{liebersohn2024} never touches the \$764.7 billion benchmark during calibration, unlike Path A's rate-gap and burnout coefficients, which are estimated directly from the Freddie Mac sample. The two are mutually reinforcing, not redundant. Path A fits the same suppression channel on the same loans ($+0.29$ per 100 bp; Section~\ref{sec:patha}). The Fannie Mae replication reproduces its sign on a second agency's loan book at roughly $3.6\times$ the per-basis-point magnitude. That is directionally consistent with the imported band, but no more. A pre-committed test of $H_0\!: \beta_g \le 0$ respecting the diagnosed bootstrap bias does not reject at any conventional level (run \texttt{patha\_sign\_test}: BCa, 296-refit jackknife, $p = 0.093$ stratum, $0.412$ temporal; 200-draw month-permutation, $p = 0.241$). The in-sample estimates are therefore not statistically distinguishable from zero under bias-respecting constructions, and the elasticity's evidential basis is the external literature alone. \citet{graybill2026} independently corroborate the band's location: a transaction-record sale-hazard response of roughly 7--11\% per percentage point of rate gap, in a housing-market-equilibrium setting touching no credit file. That corroboration comes from at or above the adopted band. Only the 7\% low end falls inside 5.5\%--7.7\%, and the midpoint is above the high edge. The central calibration is therefore conservative against this anchor as well. A prepayment-hazard coefficient is also not unit-comparable to \citepos{liebersohn2024} quarterly-mobility decline, and Path A measures prepayment, not pure mobility. What remains is directional consistency, not triangulation of sign or direction, not magnitude. A second anchor corroborates from outside the band. \citepos{fonseca2024} roughly 9\% moving-rate reduction per 100 bp, propagated through the same transform ($\beta_1 \approx 0.096$) and band machinery, yields $+\$87.9$ billion ($+11.5$ points) at the production floor. That figure is above the Liebersohn--Rothstein high edge ($+\$79.5$ billion, $+10.4$ points) yet inside the pre-committed floor-and-band box, so the central calibration is conservative among the available literature estimates. That estimand is a moving-rate reduction, not a quarterly-mobility decline, so I read it as directional corroboration, not a competing point estimate.

Path B is accordingly a structural verification exercise, not a second fit to my benchmark. Does an elasticity estimated in a different empirical setting, dropped into a PSA-baseline hazard with competing risks and no tuning against my target, reproduce the Federal Reserve's actual QT runoff shortfall? The aggregate level is floor-dominated, so landing within 8 percentage points of the benchmark is not by itself evidence for the imported elasticity: the residual the elasticity must explain over the $\beta_1 = 0$ null is itself a function of the floor calibration, and the marginal spans $+2.1$ to $+13.2$ points across the calibration box of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}. The defensible verification claim is the bounded interval that box implies. The marginal is positive at every floor, band, and floor-form point tested, but under this window's rate configuration that positivity verifies the specification. It does not identify the mechanism (Section~\ref{sec:identification}); I do not claim the level validates the elasticity's magnitude. Nor is timing part of that evidence: a zero-elasticity rerun reproduces the same three-month lead (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}), so the lead cannot discriminate the elasticity from the rate-inelastic model.

Second, propagating the full Liebersohn--Rothstein 5.5\%--7.7\% sensitivity band through the simulation yields a recovery range of \$809.9 billion to \$827.7 billion (105.9\%--108.2\% of benchmark under the standalone scorer; 96.8\%--99.1\% on the shared accounting basis of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}). The peak cross-correlation lag is stable at $-3$ across the entire band, with peak $r$ between $+0.371$ and $+0.423$. Given the moving-block intervals above and the seven-lag search, these differences carry no evidential weight. Stronger assumed mobility suppression therefore maps monotonically to more trapped liquidity, as expected, and the central 107.0\% estimate should be read as a point within this interval; Figure~\ref{fig:band} plots the band. That band is a calibration interval, not sampling error. The sampling dimension is quantified separately in Appendix~\ref{app:params}, where the committed within-stratum loan bootstrap and the stratum-cluster scheme that widens it are reported with their caveats. Even at the cluster scheme's wider interval the sampling layer is less than half the width of the floor-read interval of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}, so the operative uncertainty statements for Path B's level and marginal remain the calibration box and floor-form results there. The shared-basis equivalents follow by the common 9.1-point netting, which cancels from the marginal entirely.

\subsubsection{Path timing}

The peak alignment does \emph{not} show the simulated path leading SOMA cash flow. The empirical path moves first, and a settlement-delay mechanism---in which loan-level prepayment decisions show up in SOMA cash two to three months later---predicts the \emph{opposite} direction of offset. I therefore attach no settlement interpretation to the timing alignment; Appendix~\ref{app:patha-detail} carries the direct input-timing diagnosis behind that reading.

\subsection{External Replication on Fannie Mae Loan-Level Data}\label{sec:pathb-fannie}

Path B's construction admits a direct external replication on Fannie Mae's single-family loan performance data (24 acquisition quarters, 2017Q1--2022Q4; 17.6 million loans, 826 million loan-months). I stage it into the identical origination/performance layout, so the panel builder, estimator, and stratified 75{,}000-loan design run unchanged on the same 2017--2021 origination universe. The spec was fixed and committed before the run. Zero-balance code 01 maps to voluntary prepayment as for Freddie. Codes 06 and 16---non-credit removals with no Freddie counterpart---are censored; were they all prepayments, the worst-case bracket is 0.38\% of removals. The pre-committed gate required a positive Fannie marginal inside Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}'s Freddie floor/band envelope. It replicates: central \$821.0 billion (107.4\% of benchmark), $\beta_1 = 0$ null \$754.6 billion (98.7\%), and a lock-in marginal of $+\$66.4$ billion ($+8.68$ points of benchmark) against Freddie's $+\$70.3$ billion ($+9.20$ points)---inside the envelope. But that envelope, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}'s calibration box, spans 2.11 to 13.17 points: an 11.06-point window around a 9.20-point Freddie estimate, admitting a replication 77\% below it or 43\% above. A gate that wide fails only on a sign error, and I do not treat its passing as evidence. The agreement is a different matter: 17.6 million staged Fannie loans run through the same pipeline land 0.52 points from the Freddie point, far more than the gate demanded, and that closeness is the part of this exercise that is reassuring. What it certifies needs care. Every behavioral parameter is fixed a priori and identical across runs, and the two samples are compositionally indistinguishable (population-stability indices at or below 0.018, Appendix~\ref{app:composition}), so the 0.52-point agreement certifies pipeline determinism and cross-agency compositional stability, not external validity---which lives in the sign reproduction and staging inputs, not in the closeness. Path A's fixed-effects Fannie-cohort refit reproduces the rate-gap coefficient's sign, but at about $3.6\times$ the Freddie fit's per-basis-point magnitude and with Section~\ref{sec:patha}'s no-forward-precision holdout character, so it retains aggregate-corroboration status only. I record two ex ante scope notes. Whether Freddie's code 01 absorbs exits Fannie books under 06/16 is not settled by public documentation, so the claim is sign and envelope-conditional marginal consistency, not a hazard-level match. And Fannie's terms prohibit redistribution, so the package ships ingestion and replication code only---no Fannie data and, conservatively, no derived cells. The committed artifact carries headline statistics alone.

\subsection{Path A: Stratum-Month Discrete-Time Hazard Model}\label{sec:patha}

Path A is the aggregate corroboration leg: a stratum-month discrete-time hazard fit to Freddie Mac loan-level performance data. It recovers \$928.9 billion in aggregate, 121.5\% standalone---reported as corroboration only and excluded from every headline figure. Appendix~\ref{app:patha-detail} collects its estimation detail, diagnostics, and cross-correlation exhibits.

The same caution applies to the paths themselves: Path B's $+0.190$ lag-0 levels correlation is carried by the window's shared trend ($-0.23$ differenced, $-0.20$ linearly detrended). The ABM gives $-0.20$ and $-0.30$ (from $-0.32$ in levels), Path A $-0.21$ and $-0.29$ (from $-0.44$): detrending collapses them into a cluster between $-0.20$ and $-0.30$, none positive, so Path B's distinction is its level accuracy, not its timing. Against the i.i.d.\ $1.96/\sqrt{n}$ reference ($\pm 0.30$ at $n = 42$ detrended, $\pm 0.31$ at $n = 41$ differenced), Path A ($-0.29$) and Path B ($-0.20$) sit inside the band under both empirical-series conventions. The ABM's detrended value straddles its edge ($-0.301$ common-series, $-0.303$ own back-out). Indistinguishability verdicts therefore rest on Table~\ref{tab:estimators}'s wider moving-block bands, under which none of the three is distinguishable from zero. (In levels, trend-contaminated and reported only for completeness, the simulated path co-moves most strongly with the rate from four months earlier, the empirical path contemporaneously.) No single channel carries the offset: the $\beta_B = 0$ and $\beta_1 = 0$ ablations both leave the peak at $-3$; shifting the rate input one to three months either way never reaches lag 0 ($-1$ at best, under a two-to-three-month lead), moving recovered trapped liquidity under \$1.5 billion across the entire scan. The offset is therefore smooth fitted dynamics against a seasonal empirical series (mechanism not isolated); Path A's seasonal re-estimation moving its peak from $-3$ to $-2$ (Section~\ref{sec:patha}) is consistent with seasonality carrying part of it. Figure~\ref{fig:ccf} plots the full cross-correlations. At the cohort level the residual is structural, not compositional. Across the six 30-year cohorts spanning the SOMA low-coupon mass (2.0--4.5\%), every simulated CPR tracks the contemporaneous rate change near-mechanically: first-difference correlation between $-0.87$ and $-0.94$, five of six phase-locked at lag~0, the 4.5\% coupon peaking at lag $-6$ with a weaker $r=-0.29$, reproducing the aggregate $-0.318$. By contrast, no realized cohort speed responds distinguishably from zero at any lag within $\pm 6$ months. Those realized speeds are measured independently in the Freddie 2017--2021 performance panel and the Fannie replication cohorts, with Freddie lag-0 first-difference correlations $-0.05$ to $-0.16$, Fannie $-0.03$ to $-0.14$, all inside the $\pm 2/\sqrt{n}$ band, maximum magnitude $0.34$ over all cohorts and lags, and no coherent sign or lag. The aggregate empirical non-response is therefore not a composition artifact, nor the simulated lead a datable cohort-level phase shift. One honest scope limit travels with this exhibit: realized cohort-level monthly speeds exist only in the loan-level agency panels anchoring Path B, not in the SOMA holdings the empirical CPR is backed out of, so the cohort split runs in the Path B universe, and the estimator-level timing falsification above stays at the SOMA aggregate. A cumulative counterpart completes it. Monthly differences being seasonality-dominated, the cross-section is read in cumulated levels. Age-matched Freddie realized prepayment runs from 9.43\% annualized CPR (28.1\% of balance) in the shallowest surviving gap bucket ($[-1,0)$ points, mean coupon 6.02\%) to 5.24\% (16.4\%) at $-3$ points and deeper (mean coupon 3.37\%). That is a gradient of $+4.20$ CPR points, four-way stratum-cluster 95\% interval $[+3.59, +4.66]$, one-sided permutation $p$ 0.005. This is the one realized-data exhibit in this paper that moves in the lock-in direction. It is not corroboration of the imported elasticity's magnitude: the production hazard implies $+0.94$ points at the central $\beta_1$ across the same buckets, so the realized gradient is roughly $4.5\times$ the model's. The excess carries everything travelling with coupon: credit composition, within-window refinancing on high-coupon shallow buckets, seasoning past the age cut. Detection power is 0.68: the design could have resolved $\beta_1$'s implication, and what it found was larger and confounded. The composition limb is tested: standardizing the deep bucket to the shallow's vintage $\times$ FICO $\times$ LTV composition (22 cells, 18 of them carrying both endpoint buckets) leaves the gradient at $+3.44$ points (95\% CI $[+1.17, +5.14]$, imputed weight share $0.3\%$), an exact decomposition putting $+0.77$ of the raw $+4.20$ on composition and $+3.43$ within common cells (run \texttt{episode\_confrontation\_within}). Against the implied $+0.94$, the excess is not composition in dimensions the panel can hold fixed, loan age now among them: adding 12-month age bands to the standardization cell (29 cells, the same 18 in common; run \texttt{episode\_gradient\_age\_bands}) leaves the gradient at $+3.37$ points, and the same cut on age alone puts $+0.38$ of the raw $+4.20$ --- $9.1\%$ --- on seasoning composition, age in the cell moving total composition from $+0.77$ to $+0.85$. The age cut itself is held at production, so this stratifies above the cut rather than testing where it sits. The interval moves against the exhibit even as the point survives: on the age-augmented cell it runs $[+0.60, +4.35]$ (imputed weight share $0.5\%$), covering the implied $+0.94$ where the committed $[+1.17, +5.14]$ excluded it. On run \texttt{episode\_confrontation\_within}'s own pre-committed branch map, that moves this configuration from a gap that persists to one that closes. So the exhibit weakens without collapsing: seasoning composition is measured and small, and the gradient is no longer separated from the model's implication by its own sampling interval. I had pre-committed that at least $80\%$ of the primary selection's shallow exposure would sit in single-band vintages; the realized share is $60.6\%$: age is not merely a relabelling of vintage in this book, so the control is stronger than I expected. That $+0.94$ sits at the in-sample floor and production ramp, so I recomputed it across the headline calibration (run \texttt{episode\_gradient\_recompute}): the shortfall is not an artifact of the demoted anchor. At the $4.991\%$ floor the implied gradient is $+0.76$ under the production form and $+0.89$ under the additive one, so the realized-to-implied ratio is $5.5$ and $4.7$ against the $4.5$ at the in-sample anchor, and across the floor-by-form-by-ramp grid every finite ratio lies between $3.6$ and $12.7$. One cell is not finite: at the headline floor on a $75$~PSA ramp both endpoint buckets are fully censored, so the model implies \emph{no} cross-sectional gradient and the comparison degenerates. I had pre-committed two signs and one was wrong: the max-form gradient fell as predicted, but the additive form lowered it too rather than raising it. A floor in survival space compresses proportional differences even where it never censors. I therefore read the cross-section as a signed, significant level gradient whose composition component is measured and small---consistent with lock-in operating, still unusable as a measurement of its size: within-window refinancing on high-coupon shallow buckets stays unresolved, no field here separating it from moving, and a level gradient is not a dollar marginal. The monthly-timing nulls above stand unchanged.
\subsection{Identification: the Marginal over the Null}\label{sec:identification}

\subsubsection{The estimand}

The design does not identify the aggregate recovery level, which is floor-dominated. The $\beta_1 = 0$ null---elasticity switched off, baseline turnover held at its observed level---already recovers 85.7\% of the benchmark on the shared accounting basis at the headline off-window floor, and 88.7\% at the in-sample calibration point (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}; standalone figures in Table~\ref{tab:bases}). What it does identify is the \emph{marginal} of the central Path B run over that null, writing $R$ for recovered trapped liquidity,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:marginal}
\text{lock-in marginal} \;=\; R_{\text{central}} - R_{\beta_1 = 0},
\end{equation}
which is basis-invariant: the shared-accounting netting is common to both legs (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}). Its value moves with the baseline turnover floor, the design's dominant level-setter, and that floor is calibrated in-window on the very cohorts the marginal decomposes. The headline therefore measures that floor off that population: $+\$42.6$ billion, $+5.6$ points of the benchmark, at the defensible off-window floor of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}, and $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points across its range. In window the same difference is $+\$70.3$ billion, or $+9.2$ points. The recovery \emph{level} moves with the floor too, so I quote it at both calibrations, never a level from one floor beside a marginal from another. The central leg recovers 91.3\% of the benchmark on the shared basis at the off-window floor (88.2--93.7\% across its range) against 97.9\% at the in-window calibration; the null recovers 85.7\% against 88.7\%. The concave-gap variant, the only other leg re-run off window, recovers 99.8\% central against a 94.8\% null on the standalone scorer. Every other estimator level here---Path~A, the full-book and composed estimators, the covariate-prior and elasticity bands, the Ginnie overlay, the Danish legs---was run in window only, so any level without a floor label should be read at the in-window 4.0\% calibration, the configuration I call the \emph{in-sample calibration point} throughout. Differencing does not immunize the marginal against non-stationary baselines: the baseline multiplies the very rate-gap channel the null switches off, so a break in $h_0$ scales the marginal rather than cancelling from it. Under a $\pm 20\%$ multiplicative break from January 2023 onward, imposed identically on both legs and fitting nothing to the benchmark, it runs from $+5.0$ to $+12.1$ points around the committed $+9.2$ (bit-exact at the unit factor); a $\pm 20\%$ break in the turnover floor alone moves it the other way, to $+6.6$ through $+10.4$ points. Every perturbation leaves it inside the calibration box and positive: what survives a baseline break is box membership, not the point value.

Seven qualifications fix its evidential content. First, the identified content is the marginal's \emph{bounded range}, neither its point magnitude nor its sign: the box spans $+2.1$ to $+13.2$ points, and across the empirically plausible floor range the marginal spans $+5.0$ to $+12.6$ points over the full band, $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ at the central elasticity, moving to $+11.25$ under the competing-risks floor form. Censoring moves with the floor's level. At the headline 4.991\% off-window floor the central leg's hazard is pinned to the turnover floor in 68.8\% of the 1,683,124 evaluated loan-months and the $\beta_1 = 0$ leg in 35.8\%, against 36.3\% and 14.3\% at the in-sample calibration point; the central share runs 60.0\% to 77.3\% across that floor's defensible range. In most loan-months at the headline calibration, then, the elasticity no longer sets the hazard and its contribution to the marginal is truncated at the floor---though these are unweighted loan-month counts, not balance-weighted shares, and do not say what fraction of the dollar marginal the uncensored months carry. Wherever the null leg is pinned, switching the elasticity off cannot raise the hazard at all. The marginal is thus floor-level sensitive under the production max form: form-robust at roughly $+9$ to $+11$ points at the production floor (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}), but with no form-robust point at the off-window anchors, where the two forms return $+5.6$ and $+11.2$. That leaves the form-conditional range $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points as the defensible statement. Its endpoints are those of a measured mixture curve in the floor's strictly-involuntary share, rising to $+9.7$ points by $\omega = 0.25$ and plateauing near $+11.2$: the paper's own floor semantics point at the hull's upper region, and the $\omega = 0$ headline is the curve's conservative endpoint. Its upper end comes from the additive form at the 4.695\% floor and the band's 7.7\% high edge, whose central leg recovers 61.2\% of the benchmark; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} states that it should not be read as an equally credentialed member of the range. The hull holds each floor read at its point estimate, so it is not the paper's widest disclosed interval: propagating the floor reads' own cluster-bootstrap sampling error puts the max-form member at $+2.3$ to $+9.1$ points after wild-cluster correction (percentile read $+3.0$ to $+8.0$; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}), and leaves it open below $+4.3$ on an age-standardized read (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The floor read and the loan/stratum resample are reported side by side, not maximized over. Convolved as independent draws the pair spans $+2.8$ to $+9.0$ points (computed on the Webb rung, predating the binding-layer adjudication), and that convolution is the interval a reader who wants one number for sampling error should use. I do not substitute it for the floor read, because independence between a 2018 cohort-month ratio and a window-period composition resample is an assumption rather than a measurement. The hull's lower edge is thus narrower than the layer the paper calls binding; the two must be quoted together, not the hull alone.

\subsubsection{The floor's provenance and the off-window re-anchor}

The floor's provenance now has an out-of-agency read as well as an out-of-window one, and it does not narrow the range. The committed off-window rule, applied unchanged to the Fannie Mae cohort-month panel, returns 5.52\% on the headline cell against Freddie's 5.19\%, on 421 cohort-months against 438 (run \texttt{fannie\_floor\_read}). That panel carries the same fourteen columns and the same 2017 start, and it was staged for the external replication of Section~\ref{sec:pathb-fannie}; the Freddie panel replays every committed cell of that grid through the identical code path first. That read clears the clean band's 5.334\% top, but it is no second read of the clean leg: off window the rule covers pooled 2017--2019, whose Freddie counterpart is that 5.19\%, not the 2018 leg's 4.991\%, so the agency difference is 0.337 points of CPR (5.522\% against 5.185\%) and the rest is period. The age-standardized 5.51\% lands 0.01 points away, but it perturbs something else: an age correction to the 2018 leg itself, on 84.0\% imputed weight. The near-coincidence of the two is therefore not a second measurement of either. What they share is direction---both above the clean top, both saying the clean read understates the mature book's turnover---and that is what the clean leg cannot test on its own. The 2018 leg holds no out-of-the-money cohort-month past the twenty-four-month age cut, so its mature-seasoning test is not computable (Appendix~\ref{app:floormech}), and a test that cannot be run has not been passed. The one age contrast the leg does support is confounded by the PSA ramp. Where a mature contrast is measurable at all (two agencies, two rate regimes, three depth cuts), it runs one way in twelve cells of twelve, the age-$\geq$-24 read the higher one in each. In window that read is higher by 0.08 and 0.19 points of CPR on Freddie and Fannie, the rate configuration suppressing refinancing as on the 2018 leg; on the refi-contaminated pooled 2017--2019 period it is higher by 3.74 and 4.61 points, which bounds the correction from above without measuring it. A floor read confined to cohort-months aged twelve to twenty-four therefore understates mature baseline turnover and overstates the marginal: the error is signed against the headline, its size not priced anywhere in this paper. On the committed floor-to-marginal grid a 5.52\% floor brackets the marginal between the 5.334\% row's $+4.27$ points and the 6.0\% row's $+2.28$, below the $+4.3$ lower edge this paper quotes. I do not restate the headline on the Fannie floor: the production calibration is the Freddie one, and the two books differ in composition as well as in this read. So the out-of-agency read establishes only that the band's \emph{lower} edge is the soft one, the direction the age-standardized caveat already pointed.

That sensitivity makes the floor's provenance decisive, so Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} re-measures the floor outside the evaluation window and re-runs both legs there. The marginal lands at $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points across the defensible off-window range (inside the box, well clear of either edge) and stays positive at all twenty-seven floor-by-band cells run. The covariate-prior swing of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} is the design's largest single level sensitivity at both calibrations, and at the headline floor it exceeds the marginal: re-run at the 4.991\% floor, the two covariate endpoints span 8.2 points of recovery against the $+5.6$-point marginal. The marginal is the counter-fact. Switching the covariate block between its endpoints moves the off-window marginal by only $+0.1$ points, against $+1.9$ in-window, because both legs shift together where the floor censors more.

\subsubsection{The sign check}

The marginal's positivity in all twenty-seven cells is not the robust identified content. Over the forty window months the cohort panel covers (June 2022 through September 2025; the panel ends two months before the window does), the exposure-weighted mean coupon on the surviving book is 3.134\%, with 97.40\% of exposure at or below 4.79\% and 99.53\% at or below 5.09\%. Set those figures against a window-minimum 30-year rate of 5.2311\%. That minimum is the August 2022 monthly mean --- the window's rate dip --- not a June 2022 figure (\texttt{sign\_forcing\_stats}). The two uncovered months cannot overturn it: the book is closed at 2017--2021 originations, so no higher-coupon loan can enter, and the out-of-the-money share can only hold or rise as low-coupon cohorts season. Essentially the entire book is out of the money for essentially the entire window, so the rate gap is negative in every cell of every sweep reported here. A specification in which a more negative gap suppresses prepayment cannot then return a negative marginal at any floor or band in the box. The sign is fixed by the rate configuration, not by the estimated elasticity. What the twenty-seven-cell check verifies is accordingly the specification: it would catch a wiring error that flipped a coefficient or crossed the central and null legs, and I retain it on that footing. This is why the reported range, and not the sign, carries the paper's identified content. The band's monotone response is forced the same way: \eqref{eq:beta1} is monotone in $\delta$ and the hazard monotone in $\beta_1$ at that gap, so the ordering composes monotone maps. That ordering I retain as a wiring check on the same footing, not as identified content; the sweep's endpoints stay the convention range they are.

\subsubsection{What the design does not claim}

A second qualification: timing carries no credential. The $\beta_1 = 0$ null also peaks at lag $-3$ (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}), so the lead cannot discriminate the elasticity from the rate-inelastic model. A third qualification: the marginal's sampling precision is not the operative uncertainty. Its premise is now the cluster scheme's, not the retracted one's. At this calibration the stratum-cluster bootstrap puts the paired marginal at $[+8.27, +10.19]$ points, while the within-stratum $[+9.17, +9.23]$ understates by 30.8$\times$ (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). The calibration box, 11.1 points wide against the cluster read's 1.9, still governs the interval.

A fourth qualification concerns composition. The decomposition exhibits in this subsection, computed at the in-sample calibration point, partition the $+\$70.3$ billion figure and report shares and intensities --- quantities the floor's level shifts far less than it shifts the total. Group ablation (zeroing $\beta_1$ cell by cell, everything else at production) shows the $+\$70.3$ billion broad-based, not cohort-specific. All fifteen vintage $\times$ coupon cells are positive, they sum to within 1.03\% of the paired-run total, and no cell exceeds 21\%. The 2020--2021 vintages carry 72\% of the marginal on 73\% of balance, and per-balance intensity runs from $0.82\times$ to $1.75\times$. On the dimensions that grid misses --- FICO ($<$680 / 680--740 / 740$+$), LTV ($\leq$80 / $>$80), Census region --- the same engine returns broad-based on all three. Every cell in those partitions is positive as well, and the sums reproduce the $+\$70.3$ billion total within interaction residuals of 0.8, 0.4, and 0.5 percent. Per-balance intensity across the three lies in 0.88--1.26, tighter than the vintage $\times$ coupon span. That verdict does not, on its own, discriminate, and I say so: twelve random three-way partitions of the same 75,000 loans, run through the identical engine and pre-committed rule, return \emph{broad-based} in all twelve, on intensities of 0.995--1.007 and additivity residuals of 0.49 percent (\texttt{subgroup\_marginals\_placebo}). A partition with no economic content earns the label, so the label alone carries no evidential weight. On the benchmark basis, the 0.2-point proximity of the projection's uniform-spread 88.5\% upper end to the null's in-sample 88.7\% recovery is a doubly conditional coincidence, not corroboration. Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} withdraws that presentation, states both conditions with their magnitudes, and reports projection range and null separately; I claim no agreement from their proximity. Every percentage-point magnitude quoted for this decomposition is cap-basis, while dollars and rankings are basis-invariant.

\paragraph{Distributional incidence.} The placebo strips the homogeneity verdict of its evidential content, but a distributional finding survives it: who bears the mobility cost, not whether a label discriminates. The comparison establishes the reverse: the random partitions' intensities concentrate on unity, the widest of the twelve spans 0.011, while the economic groupings span 0.381, more than thirty times wider. The informative statement is therefore not homogeneity, which chance alone delivers, but dispersion well beyond chance: the tilts are structure, not sampling noise, and the generality claim rests on sign homogeneity and the bounded magnitude of that dispersion, not on the verdict label. Those tilts run mildly toward below-740-FICO ($1.23\times$--$1.26\times$) and above-80-LTV ($1.23\times$) borrowers, regional intensities spanning only 0.93--1.06 (Figure~\ref{fig:marginalcells}). The marginal is a book-wide property of the rate-gap distribution interacting with seasoning and the floor, so the lock-in cost falls mildly more than proportionally on weaker-credit and higher-leverage borrowers, close to evenly across regions --- a statement about where a book-wide effect concentrates, measured against a placebo that fixes what chance alone would deliver; it is not evidence of a subgroup-specific mechanism, and this design does not carry it to household welfare.

The monthly-path and coupon-bucket timing exhibits are collected in Appendix~\ref{app:patha-detail}. Neither simulated leg carries calendar-month seasonality, so seasonal structure enters these exhibits only through the empirical comparison. Per the pre-committed posture of Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}, they are fit diagnostics, not timing credentials.

A fifth qualification states what kind of claim ``identifies'' makes. The marginal is a within-model counterfactual decomposition: the difference between two runs of one fully disclosed hazard structure, differing only in the elasticity term. It is not causal identification in the econometric sense. This paper exploits no quasi-experimental variation in rate gaps across otherwise-comparable loans, and it claims none. The causal content of $\beta_1$ is inherited, not asserted: I import the elasticity unmodified from \citepos{liebersohn2024} estimated band, with \citet{fonseca2024} and the transaction-record band corroboration of \citet{graybill2026} as independent support. No Path B parameter is estimated on the benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). The switch-off adds accounting at a stated price: the marginal inherits any misspecification of the held-fixed structure. So I sweep the channels I can name --- floor level and functional form, the rate-gap transform, the covariate priors --- each directly (Sections~\ref{sec:pathb} and \ref{sec:robustness-floor}), and Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty} reports calibration, not sampling, as the operative uncertainty.

A sixth qualification concerns three readings I decline in three separate places. Path A's fitted rate-gap coefficient is $+0.29$ per 100 bp against the imported $\beta_1$'s $0.069$, on different scales. I therefore compare the two on sign not magnitude (Section~\ref{sec:patha}). The realized cross-cohort gap gradient is roughly $4.5\times$ what the central $\beta_1$ implies. The real-covariate cross-design variant recovers 59.3\% of the benchmark, 76.3\% reweighted to the book's composition, against the production ABM's 13.6\% seed mean (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}). I stand by each reason. The first is not unit-comparable and does not survive a bias-respecting sign test. By contrast, the second survives composition standardization ($+3.44$ against the implied $+0.94$, run \texttt{episode\_confrontation\_within}). I decline it on the narrower ground that remains. Within-window refinancing on shallow buckets is inseparable from moving, and a level gradient is not a dollar marginal. Finally, the third is a recovery level, which this design does not identify, and the same reweight moves the frozen cross-design variant down to 12.6\%. Stated in one place: none of the three points toward a smaller lock-in channel. I keep the imported value for Section~\ref{sec:pathb}'s reason. Path B is a structural verification exercise whose elasticity is estimated in a different setting and never touches this benchmark, so re-fitting it in-sample would end the exercise. Among available literature estimates the adopted band is already the conservative one. \citepos{fonseca2024} anchor puts the marginal at $+11.5$ points against the central calibration's $+9.2$ at the production floor. Other disclosed variants move it the other way, the Ginnie-speed overlay to $+4.4$ points and the age-standardized floor read to a measured $+3.7$ (Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}). So I do not read the three as a case for a larger headline.

\subsubsection{The assembled corrections}

A seventh qualification assembles those readings against the ones that run the other way, because this paper states each correction where it arises and never lines them up. Lined up they show what the sections do not. Holding the production hard-maximum form and the imported elasticity fixed, every correction to the floor or to the accounting basis moves the headline down; re-solved under the additive form, the calibrated companion below moves it up instead. The in-sample calibration returns $+9.2$ points. Re-anchoring the floor outside the window returns the headline $+5.6$. The Ginnie-speed overlay at that same floor returns $+4.4$. The age-standardized floor read of 5.51\% returns a measured marginal of $+3.7$, against the $+3.8$ its position on the frozen grid implies. The Fannie Mae read of the same rule on the pooled off-window period, 5.52\%, brackets the marginal below the $+4.3$ edge of the clean band. Its like-for-like Freddie counterpart is 5.19\%, not the 2018 leg's 4.991\%. The anchor convention does not flatter the headline. The middle of the five reads the 10\% support rule admits is 4.869\%, below the adopted 4.991\%, and a lower floor implies a marginal above the headline. Carrying the overlay's $0.797$ scaling up to the age-standardized floor projected a marginal near $+3.0$. The joint cell has now been run under a pre-committed landing rule (run \texttt{b5\_joint\_cell}) and measures $+2.9$ points, an interaction within $0.1$ of proportional. By contrast, this paper's first joint cell returned an interaction of $-1.47$ points (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). Corrections do not compose additively, and a composed figure would overstate precision. Nor are they independent: floor corrections and the censoring share are one mechanism read twice, since raising the floor crowds the elasticity out. At the 4.991\% headline floor the central leg is already pinned to the floor in 68.8\% of evaluated loan-months, against 36.3\% at the in-sample calibration. Further increases have progressively less hazard left to act on. That share is no claim that the estimate rests on a third of the data. A pinned month's contribution is truncated at the floor rather than erased. The $\beta_1 = 0$ leg is pinned in only 35.8\% of loan-months there, so the legs separate wherever the null clears it. These are unweighted loan-month counts rather than balance-weighted shares, so nothing follows about the uncensored months' share of the dollar marginal. What the assembly settles is where in the interval the mass sits, not whether the finding holds. Within the production form the widest disclosed layer is the baseline level. Sweeping the seasoning ramp over 75 to 150 PSA on both legs carries the marginal from $+0.9$ to $+15.6$ points at the off-window floor and $+3.3$ to $+16.5$ at the in-sample calibration. That span has no coverage property: it is a range over a convention I did not estimate, its endpoints not draws from anything. Aggregate fit is no defense of the 100~PSA convention. At the off-window floor the $\beta_1 = 0$ null recovers 101.9\% of the benchmark under the standalone scorer at 75~PSA against 94.8\% at the production 100, while the central leg is closer at 100 (100.4\%) than at 75 --- the legs rank the ramps opposite ways. A conditional estimate of the ramp's \emph{shape} now exists and does not favour the headline. Transporting the age profile of Path~A's estimated seasoning spline onto $h_0$ returns a median marginal of $+9.44$ points, 5th-to-95th-percentile spread $+8.99$ to $+9.81$, across the 107 of 200 bootstrap replicates whose normalised profile stays inside the usable range (run \texttt{h0\_reanchor}). The level is held fixed by construction, absorbed by the fitted constant and stratum fixed effects and not separately identified. Every replicate lies above the $+5.6$ this paper headlines and all but one above the $+8.7$ upper edge of the binding interval quoted below, though all lie inside the $75$--$150$~PSA span. With 46.5\% discarded it is a conditional estimate, not the ramp's sampling interval, so the convention span stays labelled unestimated. I had pre-committed to the opposite sign: that re-anchoring would push more loan-months onto the floor and so \emph{lower} the marginal. The bind share did rise as predicted, from 35.8\% to between 50.4\% and 58.2\% on the null leg, and the marginal rose anyway: a failed prediction, reported as one. The floor read itself transports in two directions, both fixed before either ran (run \texttt{floor\_transport\_two\_sided}). Standardizing its calendar raises it: the 2018 leg is read in August--December---two July cohort-months aside---on the declining side of the committed seasonal profile. Re-weighting to the window's calendar mix carries 4.991\% to 5.2156\% and the marginal to $+4.70$ points. Matching its housing-activity level lowers it: active listings averaged 1.27 million over those 2018 months against 0.80 million over the window. The same multiplicative transport carries the floor to 3.1533\% and the marginal to $+10.69$. I report the two at their own floors and do not net them: two floor-side adjustments composed with an interaction of $-1.47$ points the one time this paper ran them jointly. A cancellation would be a finding requiring its own cell, not arithmetic. The activity leg is a listings-based proxy for an argument about existing-home \emph{sales}, not constructible here (the sales series carries no observations over the 2018 leg months), and its magnitude inherits a one-for-one activity-to-turnover mapping nothing here estimates. The sign is what it establishes. Post-stratification onto the book's composition moves the marginal less than the paper's own share arithmetic implies. Raking the pool's balance weights to the SOMA coupon marginal and the book's vintage mix within the draw's 2017--2021 support (both margins hit to better than $10^{-10}$) returns $+5.11$ points against the unweighted $+5.57$, a move of $-0.47$. The vintage margin alone returns $+4.67$, so the coupon margin offsets about half the vintage correction. The vintage overlay's $+3.7$, pure share scaling, overstates the composition correction roughly fourfold. Two limits travel with it. The book's coupon-by-vintage \emph{joint} distribution is not observable (the SOMA cohort tabulation collapses vintage to one value-weighted origination date per coupon bucket), so this matches two margins, not a cell grid. The pre-2017 and 2022 vintages, 33.7\% of book face, have no draw support, are not re-weighted here, and stay with the vintage overlay. I pre-committed both a direction and a band: the direction held and the marginal fell, while $+5.11$ lies just outside the $[+2.5, +5.0]$ band. I report that miss. I do not re-draw the band. The widest layer that does have a coverage property is the floor reads' own sampling error, $+2.3$ to $+9.1$ points after wild-cluster correction of the percentile read's under-coverage (run \texttt{floor\_inference\_correction}; the percentile read is $+3.0$ to $+8.0$). That is the interval this paper quotes as binding. The headline sits just below its midpoint, every correction listed above falls in its lower half, and the uncomposed pair near its bottom. Resolution should be read off those magnitudes, not the printed decimals. Against a binding interval roughly seven points wide the weighting scheme moves an endpoint about $0.06$ of a point, so the interval is credible to something like half a point and one-decimal endpoint comparisons fall below it. The two are nested: the ramp sweep is the outer statement, $+2.3$ to $+9.1$ a sampling interval on the floor read alone, nested inside it at the production 100~PSA ramp. Which rung of that inner layer to quote is settled by the pre-committed coverage rule of run \texttt{fewcluster\_coverage}, not by width. The restricted inversion is the narrowest construction clearing the $93.0\%/91.0\%$ bar, and CR2 at Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom ($+2.4$ to $+9.1$ points), the other qualifying rung, stands beside it, not behind. A designed coverage simulation at this read's committed leverage profile (run \texttt{fewcluster\_coverage}: 31 clusters, 5.9 effective, maximum leverage 0.33; 5{,}000 draws $\times$ 999 replications) covers 94.3\% under Gaussian and 95.1\% under $t_5$ cluster disturbances for the restricted wild-cluster inversion. The comparators are 90.8\%/91.4\% for the Webb wild-$t$, below the spec's pre-committed $93.0\%/91.0\%$ qualification bar, 84.0\%/85.7\% for CR1, 89.5\%/89.9\% for the demoted percentile read, and 99.5\%/99.6\% (over-covering, and wider) for CR2 at Bell--McCaffrey. The frozen selection rule therefore lands the binding layer on the restricted inversion, whose $[+2.3, +9.1]$ this paper quotes. The Webb wild-$t$'s narrower $[+2.9, +8.7]$ is reported beside it, no longer as the binding layer. So the binding-layer choice is measured, not asserted, and the rule was applied after seeing which way it fell. What that layer prices is itself form-conditional: the same $0.99$-point rise in the floor that carries the read off window moves the marginal by $-3.63$ points under the production form and by $-0.04$ under the additive one. The interval's width is as much a property of the max form's censoring as of the floor read. Two further v20 companions price adjacent layers. Re-run on each leg's own counterfactual balance path, not renormalized monthly to realized holdings (run \texttt{compounding\_consistent\_null}), the marginal is $+4.84$ points (\$37.0 billion; central 92.3\%, null 87.4\% shared). That prices the renormalization's disclosed upper-bound bias at $0.74$ points within the quoted interval. The floor read's sampling layer propagated to the null's level puts a wild-cluster interval of $[80.6, 88.4]$\% on the 85.7\%, beside the floor-range band of Table~\ref{tab:headline}. I attach no posture to where $+5.6$ sits among these readings. With the downward variants lined up beside the upward counterweights, no interior member is privileged, and the range rather than any point is what the design delivers. The form-conditional hull of $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points remains the wider statement, since the form dimension is not a floor correction and is not disciplined by this list. Three things stop the list from becoming a retraction. The sign is fixed by the window's rate configuration, not by these calibrations, as the sign-forcing argument above establishes, so nothing here bears on the direction. Zero-exclusion is deliberately not among the three, a forced sign making it a restatement of that configuration. Specification choices that are not floor corrections run both ways, and this assembly lines both up. Upward: the additive form returns $+11.2$ points at the same off-window anchors, nearly floor-invariantly, and sets the hull's upper end. \citepos{fonseca2024} anchor would put the marginal at $+11.5$ points at the production floor against the adopted band's $+9.2$, and the housing-activity calibration below, re-solved under that form, joins them. Downward: the concave transform alone returns $+5.06$ at the off-window point floor against the production $+5.57$, and the sub-band elasticity at $\delta = 3.25\%$ returns $+3.47$ (Table~\ref{tab:lowband}). The moving-share bracket at the headline floor returns $+3.36$ points at $\mu = 0.5$ and $+1.82$ at $\mu = 0.25$ against $+5.57$ at $\mu = 1$. It is a convention bracket, not an estimate, mildly super-proportional because censored contributions do not scale with $\mu$. The survival-selection attenuation of the same imported coefficient is a bracket of the same kind and belongs here: $\eta = S_{\mathrm{pre}}^{\xi}$ at the measured pre-window survival share carries the marginal from $+5.1$ at $\xi = 0.25$ to $+3.7$ at $\xi = 1$, $\xi$ free (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}) --- a different axis from the elasticity band, not a widening of it. The assembly is not exhaustive over runs scoring a marginal at a labelled floor. The PSA sweep, the covariate ablation and the floor-form mixture do so too, reported at their own sites. I state no rule admitting these rows while excluding those. A calibrated companion prices the omitted housing-activity level term (Table~\ref{tab:specbox}) from the paper's own catalogued anchor. \citet{aladangady2024} attribute 44\% of the 2021--2022 mobility decline to rate-gap lock-in. Scaling the seasoning baseline in both legs until the non-rate channel traps $0.56/0.44$ times the rate channel's trapped liquidity puts the headline floor's root at $\phi^{*} = 0.754$ (effectively 75.4 PSA) and the marginal at $+0.9$ points at the headline floor and $+3.5$ at the in-sample calibration (run \texttt{scaled\_null\_housing\_activity}). The $\phi = 1$ cells reproduce the committed values bit-exactly. The variant documents an upward bias under both forms; under the production form the corrected member lies below the headline, at the bottom of the ramp span just swept, and at the headline floor falls outside the quoted $+2.3$ to $+9.1$ interval. That is an externally anchored calibration the quoted interval does not cover, not a challenge to the sign. At the in-sample calibration it falls inside. Re-solving the same condition under the additive form barely moves the root ($\phi^{*} = 0.756$ against $0.754$ at the headline floor, a direction I declined to predict). It lands the surviving marginal at $+8.5$ points at both anchors, clearing the $+4.0$-point bar fixed before the run (run \texttt{scaled\_null\_housing\_activity\_additive}; the $\phi = 1$ cells reproduce the committed additive legs bit-exactly). The calibration cuts the additive form's own $+11.2$ to $+8.5$ as it cuts the production form's $+5.6$ to $+0.9$. What the form decides is where the corrected member lands: above the headline at both anchors and near the top of the quoted interval under the additive form, below the headline at both under the production one. Censoring separates them: at the production form's root the central leg is pinned to the floor in 94.4\% of evaluated loan-months and the maximum truncates the elasticity there. The additive form combines the two hazards on the survival scale, so the floor, which dominates no less often at the additive root, truncates nothing. On that side of the fork this reading belongs with the counterweights above rather than with the ladder. That the floor side runs one way is also an absence. The floor is a level of total turnover, not of a strictly involuntary component (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), so it carries its period's activity. Re-reading it at the window's own level enters at the floor, not the ramp just scaled, and so cuts the other way --- a lower anchor raises the marginal. It is the one floor-side correction I can name that would move the headline up, turning on whether the window's turnover level is the lower of the two, which I do not measure here. And the three readings of the preceding paragraph point to a larger lock-in channel, not a smaller one. I decline those three for the reasons given there and decline to compose these for the reason given here: the identified content is the range. Floor and basis corrections concentrate in its lower half, and the form-side counterweights sit at its top or above it. Table~\ref{tab:assembly} tabulates this assembly.

Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty} consolidates the three uncertainty layers --- sampling, simulation seed, and calibration --- that attach to the headline level and marginal on both accounting bases. The hierarchy it displays is the one argued above: sampling error under the operative cluster schemes sits an order below the calibration layers, the draw-seed contribution is smaller still (under \$0.01 billion across ten paired reruns), and the operative uncertainty on every headline quantity is calibration. The headline marginal is the exception. There the binding layer is the floor read's own propagated sampling error (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}) --- the layer Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}'s headline row quotes, binding among the layers with a coverage property. The unestimated baseline-level convention spans wider (run \texttt{psa\_level\_sweep}, Section~\ref{sec:identification}).

\noindent{\footnotesize \emph{Notes to Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}.} Seed noise: the marginal varies by less than \$0.01 billion across ten paired draw seeds (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}). The Fannie Mae external replication reproduces the marginal at $+8.68$ points against Freddie's $+9.20$. It clears the pre-committed envelope, but that envelope is 11.06 points wide and the agreement, not the gate, is what carries the information (Section~\ref{sec:pathb-fannie}). \quad Both cluster bootstraps above rest on few clusters: 31 carry the floor read, and the loan/stratum scheme's 130 strata are unequal enough to be worth 25.8 effective ones. Percentile cluster bootstraps are known to under-cover at cluster counts in that range. This rung's printed lower edge additionally turns on an unlabelled convention: 26 of its 1{,}000 draws fall outside the committed floor grid, all 26 above the 6.0\% edge and therefore all on the low-marginal side, and the row excludes them. Truncating them to the edge instead would print $+2.28$ to $+8.01$ in place of $+3.0$ to $+8.0$. Runs \texttt{floor\_inference\_correction} and its Webb-weight successor \texttt{floor\_inference\_correction\_v2} correct the floor read. Table~\ref{tab:ladder} sets the corrected ladder out layer by layer with its degrees of freedom, and the headline row quotes the wild-$t$ interval as the binding layer among those with a coverage property. That interval prices one layer: the floor read's own sampling error around the 4.991\% point read, propagated through the frozen floor-to-marginal grid at the production hard-maximum form, the 100~PSA ramp and the central elasticity $\delta = 6.5\%$. None of its width comes from the elasticity, which is swept separately in the calibration column and in Table~\ref{tab:lowband} --- swept over the published 5.5\%--7.7\% range as a convention, not estimated, so no layer of this paper's accounting prices sampling error in the elasticity itself. Nor does any of its width come from the read-to-read spread across the floor reads in the calibration column. No such correction is run for the loan/stratum scheme, whose interval is still better read as a lower bound on sampling uncertainty than as calibrated 95\% coverage. One input to the convolved line being a lower bound makes the convolved line a lower bound as well. The ladder's reads agree in location and none crosses zero. The closest agreement among them carries no information: the Rademacher and Webb constructions land with both endpoints within a tenth of a point, a re-printing rather than a substantive move. Both are sign-symmetric reweightings of the same residuals, so each bootstrap distribution turns on the draw's sign for the one cluster carrying a third of the total leverage---nearly twice the next largest (Table~\ref{tab:ladder}). Their coincidence re-prints that cluster's influence instead of testing it. The small-sample degrees of freedom and the restricted inversion are wider. The bootstrap imposes the point estimate as the data-generating truth over unrestricted residuals (9{,}999 replications), studentizes on the floor's own scale, and maps endpoints through the frozen floor-to-marginal grid. \quad The within-run floor dispersion range is the $\kappa$ grid of Section~\ref{sec:pathb}. It bounds the marginal's sensitivity to floor variation within the window; it does not bracket floor cyclicality, and should not be read as doing so. Scrambling the floor path's time order leaves 85.0\% of its span standing, and switching the combination rule from the hard maximum to the additive form eliminates 98.0\% of it (the $\kappa$ grid then spans $+11.22$ to $+11.27$ points). Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor} diagnoses the Jensen mechanism responsible. The co-movement component that does survive is monotone in $|\kappa|$ and sign-consistent, but reaches at most \$5.29 billion in any cell. \quad The calibration catalogue for the headline marginal row --- the out-of-agency read, the form and transform variants, the overlays, and the convention sweeps --- is in Appendix~\ref{app:floormech}.\par}
