\appendix

\noindent\textbf{Online Appendix.} The appendices that follow are for online publication. The run ledger of superseded figures and the pre-committed-rule adjudication record form a standalone document in the replication package (\texttt{replication\_appendices}); its cross-references resolve against this manuscript.

\section{Agent-Based Model: Specification}\label{sec:method-abm}

I adopt an agent-based approach because the phenomenon under study is heterogeneous. Households differ in coupon, loan age, and exposure to search and sentiment frictions, and these differences interact non-linearly with the level of interest rates. \citet{geanakoplos2012} argue for, and demonstrate the feasibility of, agent-based models of mortgage prepayment in environments where behavioral and search frictions vary across the population and over time. That property is directly relevant here, since the frictions I model (housing search costs and consumer sentiment) are themselves time-varying. The broader methodological case for agent-based computational economics in settings with meaningful heterogeneity is set out in \citet{tesfatsion2006}.

The ABM simulates a population of N = 10,000 heterogeneous households. Initial agent endowments (income, home value) are drawn from log-normal distributions calibrated to Federal Reserve Economic Data (FRED) medians. In the production multi-vintage specification of Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}, each household's mortgage coupon and origination date are drawn from the Federal Reserve's actual holdings composition: the live CUSIP-level SOMA parse of Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}, across seven coupon cohorts. A single-cohort variant, used for exposition and mechanism analysis, instead endows every agent with a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage at a 3.0\% coupon aged 60 months at the simulation start. Where the text below discusses a single representative coupon, it refers to this pedagogical case, not to the production population.

Section~\ref{sec:robustness} reports a scale-sensitivity replication showing that the gap in explanatory power is not an artifact of sample size. Whether it reflects modeling paradigm or data source is taken up in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}.

The decision to relocate weighs the utility of moving against the aggregate financial penalty of doing so. The monthly payment follows the standard annuity formula
\begin{equation}\label{eq:annuity}
M = P\,\frac{r\,(1+r)^{n}}{(1+r)^{n}-1},
\end{equation}
where $P$ is the outstanding principal, $r$ is the monthly interest rate, and $n$ is the remaining term in months. The mobility penalty evaluates \eqref{eq:annuity} twice. The first evaluation applies the borrower's locked-in note rate to the original principal and term, which gives the current payment; by the amortization identity, that payment equals the payment on the outstanding balance at the note rate over the remaining term. The second applies the prevailing market rate to the outstanding balance over a fresh full original term, which gives the payment the borrower would take on by refinancing. The replacement loan re-amortizes---a term-preference persistence the code implements deliberately. The penalty scales with the difference between the two. A native short-term variant of the penalty, active only for terms at or below 240 months, floors payment decreases at zero and is specified with the full decision rule in Appendix~\ref{app:abmspec}. Here $r$ is expressed in decimal monthly terms: the annual note rate divided by twelve, so that a 3.0\% coupon gives $r = 0.0025$. It is a rate \emph{level}, distinct in both role and units from the annualized rate \emph{gaps} $g_{i,t}$ (in 100 bp units) and $\tilde g_{i,t} = g_{i,t}/100$ (decimal) that drive the hazard framework of Section~\ref{sec:pathb}. No rate symbol is reused across the two. I evaluate the population's move/stay decisions household-by-household at each point of a three-dimensional grid of market rate, dynamic friction, and rate velocity (and, in the multi-vintage specification, across seven coupon cohorts), then aggregate them to a pool-level annualized Conditional Prepayment Rate (CPR) surface. CPR is a pool-level convention; the household-level object is an annualized move intensity, consumed by the accounting layer at one-twelfth per month (Appendix~\ref{app:abmspec}). That surface is precomputed once and then interpolated month-by-month against the live population of simulated market conditions, which cuts the cost of the full grid by roughly two orders of magnitude and makes fifty-seed Monte Carlo estimation feasible. The surface stores each node's direct household-by-household evaluation, so interpolation reproduces the micro-simulation exactly at grid points. The operative approximation is the trilinear segment between 50 bp-spaced nodes. A spot-check validates that approximation directly: after an on-grid parity gate reproduced the committed surface values, direct micro-simulation and interpolation at worst-case off-grid midpoints differ by at most 0.23 points of CPR away from the freeze threshold, with the Danish column exact. The one systematic difference is the wait-and-see freeze itself. The engine applies it as a step at the 150 bp trigger, while the surface renders it as a ramp across the adjacent velocity nodes. That gap binds in four realized months (August--November 2022; SOMA-weighted mean absolute difference 0.22 points of CPR over the window). These are surface semantics that the production figures share, and the binary-step leg of Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions}'s freeze sweep prices their trapped-liquidity consequence directly. Figure~\ref{fig:cprsurface} renders one slice of that surface (the 3.0\% cohort of the expository variant) with the realized QT window located on it.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{fig8_cpr_surface}
\caption{The precomputed CPR surface: the production surface's slice at the 3.0\% thirty-year SOMA cohort, at its parsed seasoning. Left: the U.S. par-payoff surface over the market-rate $\times$ dynamic-friction grid (no-freeze regime), with the 42 QT-window months overlaid at their realized (rate, friction) coordinates; the window sits entirely in the deep out-of-the-money corner, near the baseline turnover floor. Right: rate slices at the friction grid point nearest the window mean. Velocity enters the grid only through the wait-and-see freeze of Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions} (20\% of otherwise-mobile households held in place above a 150 bp trailing six-month rise), so the two regimes shown are its entire velocity structure; the Berger-recalibrated Danish counterfactual (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}) is near-flat, moving roughly a quarter point across the grid. The U.S. slice is identical across the frozen production surface and the current code head; the Danish curve is the Berger production specification.}
\label{fig:cprsurface}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{fig10_abm_cpr_paths}
\caption{Production ABM simulated CPR against the empirical SOMA back-out over the QT window (frozen fold-in run, seed 42). The simulated path runs at roughly twice the empirical level and does not track its month-to-month shape; the annotated means, lag-0 $r$, and $R^2$ are the manifest values quoted in the text. This is the level-and-shape mismatch behind the negative path statistics; the cross-correlation structure across lags is in Figure~\ref{fig:ccf}.}
\label{fig:abmpaths}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.82\textwidth]{monte_carlo_trapped_liquidity}
\caption{Monte Carlo population-draw distribution of ABM trapped liquidity (50 seeds, fold-in specification; CPR surface rebuilt per draw). The seed-level dispersion (SD \$24.5 billion) dwarfs the distance between the frozen seed-42 production draw (\$91.0 billion, 34th percentile) and the seed mean (\$103.7 billion); the empirical benchmark of \$764.7 billion lies far outside the distribution's support, which is the visual form of the ABM's 10--17\% recovery band.}
\label{fig:mc}
\end{figure}

The simulation contrasts two institutional payoff rules. Under U.S. par-payoff, the cost to retire the mortgage is strictly equal to the outstanding principal. This is the negatively convex case: the investor's upside is capped, while extension risk is bounded only by the scheduled-amortization profile---a 14.7-year weighted-average life at zero CPR against the no-shock 3.4-year life (Table~\ref{tab:wal}), an order-of-magnitude extension ceiling. Under Danish market-value repurchase, the payoff cost is the present value of the remaining scheduled payments discounted at the current 30-year primary mortgage rate (FRED MORTGAGE30US), capped at par. Two qualifications attach to this proxy. First, it is an option-free present value discounted at the primary rate, so it ignores the embedded par call and any option-adjusted spread; the primary rate also embeds the primary--secondary spread (guarantee fee, servicing, originator margin; \citealp{fuster2013}), whereas the bond-market discounting a true buyback would use sits below it. Both choices overstate the borrower's buyback discount as rates rise. These biases inflate the mechanism-substitution Danish CPR of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} (the since-superseded 47.1\%), but they are exactly immaterial to the Berger-recalibrated production finding. Under neither transplant anchor does that leg's prepayment hazard consume this pricing proxy: the Danish-level anchor imports the near-flat moving curve, and the production U.S.-intercept anchor evaluates the U.S. hazard at zero rate gap. A replication-package diagnostic that re-runs the Danish leg with the discount rate shifted down by 25--150 bp moves trapped liquidity by \$0.00 billion. It flips 2,470--92,495 of the 1,683,082 loan-month present-value classifications\footnote{This Danish-leg classification universe of 1{,}683{,}082 loan-months is 42 fewer than the 1{,}683{,}124 evaluated in the U.S.\ production hazard panel (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}): the two figures count active loan-months in the two counterfactual legs, whose prepayment paths differ marginally even under the shared imported elasticity,} that no downstream quantity in that leg consumes. Second, what the Danish rule neutralizes is extension risk for the bondholder \citep{berger2026}: the borrower retains the par call, so the rate-decline side keeps its price cap, and I use ``convexity-neutral'' below strictly in this rate-rise sense. Under the Danish rule, as market rates rise above 3.0\%, the bond's present value falls, which lets the borrower extinguish the debt at a discount and guarantees continuous cash flow generation. I simulate the Danish counterfactual balance forward on a dynamic, declining-balance basis: each month's elevated Danish roll-off applies to the already-shrunk balance carried over from the prior month, so the simulated payoff path compounds correctly over the multi-year QT window.

\section{Macroeconomic Friction and Structural Extensions}\label{sec:method-frictions}

To quantify the behavioral friction gap in standard models, I scale base transaction friction using two FRED series. (1) I measure search friction as the shortfall in Active Listing Count (ACTLISCOUUS) against a 2017--2019 baseline, the last stable pre-pandemic period before the 2020/21 rate-driven frenzy and the 2022 inventory collapse. (2) I index psychological friction from University of Michigan Consumer Sentiment (UMCSENT) against a baseline of 85.0, scoring readings below that line as incremental friction at +5 bp per point short, capped at +150 bp during acute sentiment stress. The result is a dynamic friction parameter---a one-time moving transaction cost expressed as a share of the household's current home value---that scales between 8.23\% and 9.96\% of home value over the 42 active QT months.

I add three behavioral extensions to the household decision function. No parameter in any of them is tuned to match the empirical benchmark. Their grounding is nonetheless uneven: the first two are parameterized from external sources, the third is an author calibration. The first is a debt-to-income (DTI) constraint that vetoes any move in which the new housing payment would exceed 43\% of gross monthly income. That threshold is a front-end heuristic motivated by the pre-2021 General QM debt-to-income limit (12 C.F.R. \S{} 1026.43(e)(2)(vi)). The regulatory threshold was a back-end total-debt ratio and was superseded by the price-based General QM definition in 2021, so I use it as a behavioral anchor. The model carries no total-debt data against which to test the threshold (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}). The second is asymmetric loss aversion that scales perceived payment increases by 2.25$\times$ while leaving decreases unscaled (loss aversion: \citealp{kahneman1979}; the 2.25 coefficient: \citealp{tversky1992}). \citepos{andersen2022} housing-market estimate of roughly 2.5 is close. Any coefficient in this range triggers the same turnover-floor recalibration described below, so the artifact interpretation of Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} does not hinge on the choice. The third is a wait-and-see freeze that holds 20\% of otherwise-mobile households in place whenever the trailing six-month change in mortgage rates exceeds 150 bp. It is modeled as a fixed per-agent trait, so the CPR surface remains deterministic. The 20\% share and 150 bp trigger are author calibrations with no external source, as are the sentiment-to-friction mapping and the base transaction-friction level of the preceding paragraph: the UMCSENT and ACTLISCOUUS inputs are data, but the $+5$ bp-per-point mapping, its $+150$ bp cap, and the base friction level are mine. Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} tests an externally parameterized variant of the mobility-desire scale and the freeze share, drawn from the same source as Path B's elasticity.

Loss aversion steepens the penalty function facing every household. The model's mobility-desire parameter is independently calibrated to reproduce a real-world baseline turnover floor of 4--5\% CPR at 8\% market rates. That floor is the turnover speed observed on deeply out-of-the-money discount cohorts during 2023--2024, when refinancing incentives were absent and remaining prepayment reflected death, divorce, and forced relocation. I therefore hold that empirical floor fixed and re-run the calibration under the steeper penalty function. The re-run requires nearly tripling the mobility-desire scale parameter, and this mechanical recalibration is the principal driver of the results in Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}.

Whenever a specification change alters the penalty or covariate structure facing every household, the mobility-desire scale, and only that parameter, is re-derived so that the simulation continues to reproduce the empirically observed 4--5\% baseline turnover floor. The same rule generates the recalibrated variant of the cross-design test (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}), whose frozen variant is defined by suspending it; Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions} reports how much work this single choice does. Because the 4--5\% floor is imposed by construction, the ABM's agreement with observed discount-cohort turnover at deep rate gaps is an input, not a finding. The model earns no evidential credit on that dimension; its evidential exposure lies entirely on the dimensions the anchor leaves free, the dollar recovery and the timing of the CPR path. The anchor is also insulated from the behavioral gates by construction: the calibration point is evaluated at zero rate velocity, where the wait-and-see freeze cannot bind, so no freeze parameterization can move the floor (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions} verifies the calibrated scale and floor CPR are bit-identical at every freeze share swept). What the anchor purchases, and what dropping it costs, is measured directly in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}: the externally parameterized variant that removes the floor overshoots the benchmark by half while predicting essentially zero turnover at QT-window rate gaps.

I added an income-scaled curtailment channel as an extension specified ex ante. The ABM's monthly CPR over-prediction correlates with year-over-year growth in real disposable income (FRED series DSPIC96) at $r = -0.487$, and the correlation concentrates in the worst over-prediction months (June--September 2022). That diagnostic led me to predict ex ante that curtailment, a strictly additive channel, would worsen the aggregate match. I implemented curtailment as an income-scaled addition to simulated roll-off, mapping real income growth linearly from zero curtailment at $-2$\% growth to a full-scale annualized rate of 1.2\% CPR at +4\% growth, consistent with GSE daily prepayment report estimates.

Finally, as a diagnostic, I replaced the single representative 3.0\% coupon assumed elsewhere with a multi-vintage composition. Bucketing SOMA holdings into seven coupon cohorts yields a weighted-average coupon of approximately 2.55\% on the 30-year book, with the 2.0\% and 2.5\% buckets accounting for roughly 69\% of 30-year face value. A lower coupon implies a larger gap to prevailing market rates, so my ex ante hypothesis was that this composition would increase predicted trapped liquidity toward the empirical benchmark. This pass excludes fifteen-year MBS (approximately 9\% of SOMA face value), and cohort origination dates are approximated from parsed maturity strings under a 360-month term assumption. Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} describes the production replacement of this diagnostic with a live, CUSIP-level pull of SOMA holdings.

\section{ABM Decision Rule and Parameter Provenance}\label{app:abmspec}
% Sources: abm/abm_lockin_simulation.py ("S:"), abm/fed_mbs_extension_risk.py ("F:"),
% abm/cross_design_test.py, abm/abm_external_gates.py. Line pins refer to the committed code.

This appendix specifies the ABM's move decision and every behavioral parameter in the detail needed to reimplement the model without consulting the code. Each equation and value is pinned to its source line in the replication package.

\paragraph{The move decision.}
Let $A(P,r,n) = P\,(r/12)\big/\big(1-(1+r/12)^{-n}\big)$ be the monthly payment on principal $P$ at annual rate $r$ over $n$ months. % S:130-136
Household $i$ carries gross income $y_i$ (\$/year), home value $H_i$ (\$), original principal $P_i = 0.8\,H_i$ (a fixed 80\% origination LTV in the synthetic population), note rate $c_i$, original term $n_i$ in months, and outstanding balance $K_i$ (closed form at the cohort's seasoning; the U.S. par payoff). % S:141-155, S:314
The replacement-loan payment is $M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t} = A(K_i, m_t, n_i)$: the par payoff financed at the market rate $m_t$ over a fresh \emph{full original term}, not the remaining term (term-preference persistence). % S:222-225, S:366-381
With $\Delta_{i,t} = M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t} - A(P_i, c_i, n_i)$, whose second term is the current payment (by the amortization identity, equal to the payment on $K_i$ at $c_i$ over the remaining term), the loss-aversion-scaled penalty, in \$/month, is term-dependent:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abmpenalty}
\pi_{i,t} =
\begin{cases}
\lambda\,[\Delta_{i,t}]_{+} + [\Delta_{i,t}]_{-} & n_i > 240 \text{ (30-year book)},\\
\lambda\,\big[M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t} - A(K_i, c_i, n_i)\big]_{+} & n_i \le 240 \text{ (native short-term gate)},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
% S:383-409; the 240-month switch is S:408
with $\lambda = 2.25$, $[x]_{+} = \max(x,0)$, and $[x]_{-} = \min(x,0)$. Payment increases are scaled by $\lambda$. On the 30-year book, decreases pass through unscaled as gains, while the short-term gate floors them at zero to isolate the pure rate-lock cost. The frozen production headline draws 15-year voluntary CPR from the same-coupon 30-year surface, so the short-term branch is operative only in the Berger-run calibration stage (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}).
The effective transaction rate is $\tau_{i,t} = \mathrm{clip}\big(\phi_t + (\tau_i - 0.07),\,0.02,\,0.12\big)$: the agent's idiosyncratic draw enters as an offset around the 7\% base mean, then is re-clipped after the macro friction $\phi_t$ shifts it. % S:241-243, S:459-461
Household $i$ moves in month $t$ iff
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abmmove}
\underbrace{\frac{M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t}}{y_i/12} \le 0.43}_{\text{DTI veto}}
\;\wedge\;
\underbrace{D_i > S\,\pi_{i,t} + \tau_{i,t} H_i}_{\text{cost--benefit}}
\;\wedge\;
\underbrace{\neg\big(v_t > 0.015 \,\wedge\, u_i < 0.20\big)}_{\text{wait-and-see freeze}},
\end{equation}
% Gates: DTI S:455-457, cost-benefit S:458-463, freeze S:464-467, conjunction S:468
where $D_i \sim \mathrm{Exponential}(\theta)$ is the mobility-desire draw in dollars, $S = 60$ months is the expected-stay horizon converting the monthly penalty into a dollar stock, and $v_t$ is the trailing six-month mortgage-rate change (decimal). The remaining term, $u_i \sim U[0,1]$, is a fixed per-agent trait --- the code's ``patience'' draw, consumed only by the freeze gate. It is not a time-preference parameter. % S:48, S:192, S:302-309
All inequalities are as coded: the DTI gate passes at exactly 43\%, the cost--benefit gate requires strict exceedance, and the freeze binds only strictly above 150 bp. At an evaluation point, the share of a pool passing \eqref{eq:abmmove} is read as that point's annualized CPR --- the calibration anchor is an annual floor --- and the macro accounting layer consumes it at $\mathrm{CPR}/12$ per month. % F:957

\paragraph{Parameter provenance.}
Table~\ref{tab:abmparams} collects every behavioral parameter. None is tuned to the \$764.7 billion benchmark. The mobility-desire scale $\theta$ is the one parameter re-derived under the floor rule, and only ever that one.

\paragraph{Order of operations.}
Within a simulated month, the three gates of \eqref{eq:abmmove} are evaluated on the surviving population and combined conjunctively. They are side-effect-free, so their order cannot change the result; the code evaluates them in vectorized form in the order DTI, cost--benefit, freeze. % S:449-468
In cohort-path (burnout) mode, movers are removed permanently before the next month's evaluation, so the turnover tail sets a natural floor on the depleted pool. % S:470-518
Two named components are not in-month gates. The 4--5\% floor enters once, before simulation, through the desire-scale search of Table~\ref{tab:abmparams}. That search is re-run, for that parameter only, whenever a specification change alters the penalty or covariate structure facing every household. Because the anchor is evaluated at zero velocity, no freeze parameterization can move it (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}). % S:649-685, S:520-524
Curtailment never touches the household decision: it is an additive term in the macro layer's simulated roll-off. Nor does production re-solve the decision rule monthly. The CPR surface is precomputed on the rate $\times$ friction $\times$ velocity grid, with all gates applied at each node, and then interpolated month-by-month; Appendix~\ref{sec:method-abm} discloses the freeze's step-versus-ramp semantics. % F:873-895, S:526-548

This paper earlier described the mobility-desire distribution as ``log-normal''; it is exponential, on a dollar scale, and the correction is recorded in the replication package's run ledger.

\noindent{\footnotesize \emph{Notes to Table~\ref{tab:terminology}.} Rows are ordered by how badly a conflation would mislead, most damaging first. ``Cluster'' is deliberately absent: it carries one sense here, the resampling unit of a cluster-robust bootstrap, and what varies is the unit, not the word --- the four-way panel stratum, the three-way draw stratum, the six calendar months of the off-window leg, and the 137 cells where those two intersect. The stratum and cell rows fix each.\par}

Table~\ref{tab:params} collects every Path B parameter. All values are fixed a priori in the configuration, and none is estimated on, or tuned to, the \$764.7 billion benchmark. One parameter is nonetheless measured inside the evaluation window, and benchmark-independence should not be read as window-independence. The 4\% baseline turnover floor is anchored to turnover observed on discount cohorts during 2023--2024 (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions} and Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), so it is in-sample with respect to the QT window even though it never touches the benchmark dollar figure. That floor sets much of the aggregate level, binding in 36\% of loan-months. Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} sweeps it jointly with the elasticity band: the aggregate level and the lock-in marginal both move materially with the floor, within the bounded interval that section reports. The marginal's sign does not move, but under this window's rate configuration it could not (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). The covariate and default-hazard coefficients are round calibration priors. Both prepayment covariates carry signs opposite the canonical credit-risk direction (higher FICO $\Rightarrow$ better refinancing access $\Rightarrow$ faster prepayment; higher LTV $\Rightarrow$ less equity $\Rightarrow$ slower prepayment). In the deeply out-of-the-money 2022--2025 window the refinancing channel is shut and prepayment is turnover-dominated, so credit covariates proxy mobility, and high-LTV loans are disproportionately recent purchases that churn. A Path-A-style estimation on the same stratum-month panel yields point estimates consistent with the priors' direction; taken at face value, they are roughly double the priors' magnitudes. That estimation is a Poisson PML with vintage-by-coupon fixed effects, so the credit buckets are identified from cross-bucket variation, and the bucket-mean covariates are z-scored to the microsim pool's own moments. But the intervals reject neither zero nor the opposite signs: $\hat{\beta}_F = -0.39$ (four-way stratum-cluster bootstrap 95\% CI $[-1.51, +0.65]$) and $\hat{\beta}_L = +0.21$ ($[-0.59, +1.00]$). The estimation does not settle the sign question; it fails to contradict the priors while quantifying their sensitivity. The sensitivity brackets are correspondingly modest: a $\beta_F = \beta_L = 0$ rerun moves the recovery from 107.0\% to 108.3\%, and the panel-estimated pair moves it to 101.3\%. Those are in-window figures. At the off-window floor the same endpoints span 8.2 points and `modest' does not carry over to the level, while the marginal's covariate sensitivity shrinks to $+0.1$ points. Benchmark-independence is unaffected, since the estimation touches only the Freddie panel. The $\beta_B = 0$ rerun of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} quantifies the burnout channel the same way.


Delinquent loans transition monthly through the matrix of Table~\ref{tab:delinq}. I estimate it from consecutive stratum-month \emph{modal} servicer states in the Freddie cohort panel, exposure-UPB weighted, and commit it as an artifact in the replication package. The default-hazard draw and the transition matrix divide the labor. The draw moves loans \emph{into} the pipeline (Current $\to$ D30) and terminates already-delinquent loans it hits. The matrix governs month-to-month movement \emph{within} the pipeline. The matrix runs after the draw each month, so a loan entering D30 via the draw can also take a matrix transition in its entry month. The \$0.39 billion extreme-matrix test below bounds that overlap along with everything else. Only the delinquency rows are used in simulation. Terminal states are absorbing by construction. The forbearance state is unpopulated in the estimation panel and never entered in production runs.
I publish the full crosstab. D30 row (21 transitions, \$58.3M row exposure): 13 cures carrying 95.6\% of the exposure weight, two D30 continuations (0.8\%), five D60 progressions (3.1\%), and one Defaulted transition (0.4\%); the printed 0.004 is the exposure share of that single event, whose count share would be $1/21 = 0.048$. D60 row (9 transitions, \$3.1M): six cures carrying 56.8\% and three D90+ progressions carrying 43.2\%, against count shares of $6/9$ and $3/9$. That divergence between count and exposure shares means a few larger-balance stratum-months drive the matrix, which is what an exposure-weighted estimator is designed to do. D90+ row (38 transitions, \$76.2M): seven cures (30.3\%), one D30 (0.8\%), thirty self-continuations (68.9\%), and zero Defaulted events; the ``--'' cells are observed zeros in a thin panel, not structural zeros, which is why serious delinquency shows no direct default exit while D30 shows one. Probabilities are printed to three decimals from these exposure shares and rows may not sum to exactly one. The matrix is accordingly reported for completeness, not precision: with cells this thin it describes the estimation panel and does not estimate a transition law. The paper's estimates rely on the materiality bound below, not on the matrix's point values.

The thinness is immaterial for the paper's estimates: replacing the entire matrix with either extreme --- all delinquent loans cure next month, or all default next month --- moves trapped liquidity by \$0.39 billion (0.05\% of the estimate). The matrix routes the 482 loans delinquent at the window start from the first month, mostly curing them, given the cure-heavy rows. Thereafter the month-end delinquency stock never exceeds 154 of the 40,234 active loans, and delinquent UPB never exceeds 1.1\% of the pool. Both extreme-matrix runs include the initial 482, so the bound covers them.

Sampling design: the draw is not a probability sample of the SOMA book, and the allocation rule is why. A pool built at four times the target size takes an equal allocation from each quarterly Freddie origination file. The pool is then drawn down to 75,000 loans proportionally within stratum, and every retained loan carries weight one (\texttt{hazard/loan\_sample.py}). The draw's stratum is the three-way vintage $\times$ coupon $\times$ FICO cell --- the Path A estimation stratum with the LTV axis dropped --- which is why the draw carries 130 of them against the panel's 296. Nothing in that chain reweights toward the book. Each origination year from 2017 through 2021 therefore takes 20.0\% of the draw by count, so 2017--19 is 60.0\% of it against 6.0\% of book face. But that count basis is not the weighting the aggregation applies. The aggregation weights by surviving balance at the window open, and on that basis the picture is materially different: 2017--19 is 27.0\%, 2020 30.6\% and 2021 42.3\%, against book face shares of 6.0\%, 16.3\% and 43.9\%. So the 2021 cohort is close to matched, and the 2017--19 overweight is a factor of about four and a half, not the factor of ten the count basis suggests; origination balance, which compares originations to current face and is the wrong basis for this comparison, gives 55.5\%, 22.2\% and 22.3\%. What no weighting repairs is out-of-support mass. The draw spans 2017 through 2021 only, so pre-2017 (10.6\% of book face) and the 2022 cohort (23.1\%) carry no support at all: 33.7\% of face, which must be netted against the Ginnie share on the book's own joint cells, not added to it. The draw's 3.86\% unweighted mean note rate also sits above the book's 2.49\% face-weighted pass-through coupon. Both tilts move the rate-gap distribution the floor takes its maximum against under the production floor form, and this paper signs neither: Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}'s coupon reweight rescales the aggregate level and leaves the marginal unchanged, so it does not test a composition effect running through the floor's bind share.

Attrition accounting: of the 75,000 sampled loans, 34,734 prepaid and 32 defaulted before the QT window opens, so 40,234 are active at the June 2022 start (39,752 current, 482 delinquent). The production U.S. run evaluates 1,683,124 loan-months over the 42-month window, a mean of 40,074 active loans per month. The baseline turnover floor $\underline{h}$ binds in 36\% of those loan-months (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).

The $\delta$-to-$\beta_1$ conversion of \eqref{eq:beta1} is insensitive to the turnover proxy behind it. In the small-$P_q$ limit the transform reduces to $\beta_1 = -\ln(1-\delta) = 0.0672$ at the central $\delta$. Over $P_q \in (0, 0.12]$---from arbitrarily low turnover to double the adopted proxy---the central $\beta_1$ ranges only from $0.0672$ to $0.0701$. Even a large error in $P_q$ therefore moves the conversion by a few percent.

\paragraph{Production step order, clamp, and parameters.}
Each month, both hazards are evaluated for every active loan. In the production configuration prepayment is fractional: each active loan's balance is drained deterministically by $h^{\mathrm{prep}}$ (a per-loan uniform draw for prepayment is a non-production discrete branch). Default instead turns on a uniform draw, which sends hits into the delinquency pipeline (current loans to D30, already-delinquent loans to default), where they transition through the Markov delinquency-resolution matrix of Appendix~\ref{app:params}. A proportional rescaling guards the case $h^{\mathrm{prep}} + h^{\mathrm{def}} > 1$. It is a safety clamp, not a competing-risks construction. In production it never binds: across all 1,683,124 evaluated loan-months per regime the maximum hazard sum is 0.016, two orders of magnitude below the threshold. At that level the exact exponential cause-allocation construction $1-\exp(-(\lambda_p+\lambda_d))$ with shares $\lambda_p/(\lambda_p+\lambda_d)$ differs from mine only at second order in the hazards. In the taxonomy of standard competing-risks formulations \citep{putter2007}, Path B is a discrete-time multi-state model parameterized entirely through cause-specific hazards. Equations~\eqref{eq:pathB} and \eqref{eq:default} give monthly event probabilities conditional on the loan remaining active---the estimand of discrete-time cause-specific regression, as implemented in open-source packages such as PyDTS \citep{meir2025}---and the delinquency pipeline of Appendix~\ref{app:params} extends the two-cause structure to a multi-state process with cures. No subdistribution (Fine--Gray) hazard \citep{fine1999} appears in the specification, and none is needed: that device places covariate effects directly on cumulative incidence functions, whereas the simulation obtains every cumulative-incidence functional (prepaid UPB, the delinquency stock, trapped liquidity) by forward composition of the cause-specific hazards. One caveat qualifies the mapping. In production, prepayment is a fractional balance-flow intensity, not a per-loan absorbing transition, so the cause-specific reading is exact for default and holds at the balance-aggregate level for prepayment. Prepaid balances settle to simulated SOMA cash flow in the month they occur; no settlement delay is modeled on this path.

\paragraph{Cohort-level burnout.}
Burnout is deliberately cohort-level, not per-loan: a fractional burnout measure is not well-defined for a one-time, absorbing transition (Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}). Each stratum instead tracks its cumulative prepaid UPB as a share of its original UPB---the $B \in [0,1]$ state defined under \eqref{eq:pathB}---after all prepayment transitions in a given month. That state is broadcast to all of that stratum's remaining active loans before the next month's hazard is evaluated, and enters \eqref{eq:pathB} with the calibrated prior coefficient $\beta_B = -0.5$ (Appendix~\ref{app:params}). Path A's separately estimated burnout coefficient, statistically indistinguishable from zero (Section~\ref{sec:patha}), plays no role here. Because the simulated burnout state is endogenous, I test whether the channel is load-bearing with a $\beta_B = 0$ rerun on the production seed: trapped liquidity moves by \$6.58 billion ($-0.86$pp of benchmark, to 106.17\%), with the peak lag unchanged at $-3$. Two qualifications travel with that figure. It is measured at the in-window 4.0\% floor; at the 4.991\% off-window floor that the paper headlines, the same ablation is worth only \$3.48 billion ($-0.45$pp), because the floor's bind share rises from 36.3\% to 68.8\% of evaluated loan-months and the hard maximum makes burnout inert wherever it binds. And because the burnout state is endogenous to realized prepayment, the ablation moves the $\beta_1 = 0$ null as well as the central leg, so the identified marginal is not burnout-invariant: switching the coefficient off raises it from $+9.20$ to $+9.94$ points at the production floor and from $+5.57$ to $+6.51$ at the off-window floor. Both moves sit inside the $\pm 1$-point materiality convention, the second only barely, so I report them without resting anything on them. The FICO and LTV covariates receive the same treatment. A $\beta_F = \beta_L = 0$ rerun moves the recovery to 108.3\% ($+1.3$pp), and a rerun under coefficients estimated Path-A-style from the panel moves it to 101.3\% ($-5.7$pp), both at the in-window 4.0\% floor. At the 4.991\% off-window floor the same endpoints span more, not less: 102.5\% against 94.3\% standalone, an 8.2-point swing. The marginal they bracket nevertheless moves by only $+0.1$ points, against $+1.9$ in-window, because both legs shift together where the floor censors more. Appendix~\ref{app:params} reports the estimation, whose imprecise point estimates are consistent with the priors' turnover-regime signs without rejecting alternatives. Production retains the ex-ante priors---the estimates are too imprecise to license replacing a pre-specified calibration mid-stream---and I flag the covariate block as the specification's largest single sensitivity: the two endpoints correspond to 99.2\% and 92.2\% on the shared accounting basis (the common netting makes the conversion exact), a swing comparable in size to the lock-in marginal itself.

\paragraph{The time-varying Ginnie overlay.}
The static Ginnie bound is now extended to a time-varying overlay built from the published series the bound's two snapshots came from. I extract the full monthly agency CPR series (June 2017 through November 2025) from the December 2025 Global Markets Analysis Report and score the 20.4\% Ginnie face share by the observed Ginnie speed in \emph{both} Path B legs. That report's aggregation was revised in that issue to weighted-average unpaid principal balance, one consistent dollar-weighted methodology across the whole window. The overlay moves the shared-basis central from 97.9\% to 89.3\% and the null from 88.7\% to 81.9\% in the raw construction, both legs at the in-sample calibration point. It confines the identified marginal to the conventional share: $+7.3$ points, positive under every variant tested (total-CPR, voluntary-only, and placebo), scaling with the conventional face share almost exactly ($0.797$ against $0.796$). Composed with the off-window headline calibration, the overlay pair is 84.0\%/79.5\% central/null on the shared basis with a $+4.4$-point marginal, and the overlay marginal is pure conventional-share scaling ($0.797\times$) --- a change of estimand, a re-scoring onto the conventional sub-book, identical across the primary, voluntary-only, and placebo series. It is not a correction to the headline: the headline marginal therefore carries the conventional-extrapolation convention wherever quoted, with $+4.4$ points its overlay-scored member. The raw level shift overstates the Ginnie-specific effect, though. A placebo that scores the same 20.4\% share by the observed GSE-mean series moves the level by $+\$27.8$ billion on its own: the model's level calibration plus the full-universe-versus-SOMA composition difference, common to any observed-series overlay. The Ginnie-specific differential contribution is therefore $+\$38.5$ billion, 5.0\% of benchmark. That sits inside the static \$20--\$47 billion bound above, and is consistent with the window-mean Ginnie--Freddie differential of 2.1 points the series itself shows against the bound's 1.2--2.8-point snapshots. The placebo and Ginnie-specific components, $\$27.8 + \$38.5 = \$66.3$ billion, reconcile with the raw 8.6-point shared-basis shift on the \$764.7 billion benchmark, $\approx\$65.8$ billion, to within rounding of the 0.1-point recovery shares. The full-universe series if anything overstates the correction for SOMA's seasoned, low-coupon Ginnie holdings, the same signed direction as the static bound; and structural Ginnie inclusion remains out of scope for the modeling reason above: the overlay treats the buyout-inclusive Ginnie speed as data, which is what runoff accounting requires of a passthrough holder. This overlay and the age-standardized floor read of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} cut the same way, and the joint cell has now been run under a pre-committed landing rule (run \texttt{b5\_joint\_cell}): composed, they return $+2.9$ points ($+\$22.4$ billion), within $0.1$ points of the proportional projection, and that measured point is the assembly's lower member. Pure conventional-share scaling is what a gap-inert Ginnie share gives. Scoring that share at a response instead (run \texttt{ginnie\_overlay\_attenuated}) prices it at a fixed proportion of the conventional share's per-unit-face wedge (transplanted, not estimated on Ginnie collateral), with the wedge fixed by requiring that a full-strength Ginnie response return the un-overlaid marginal. What the series measures is a level, and a voluntary-only one: across the window's 42 months Ginnie's voluntary speed runs $1.35$ points above Freddie's, $1.228$ times as fast, against the 2.1-point total-CPR differential above, and read off the same full-universe series with the same overstatement. An elasticity is a slope, and nothing here maps a level onto one, so I bracket the mapping rather than choose it. The four are: zero (the committed overlay); $81.4$\% of the conventional response, the reciprocal of that speed ratio, on which a book running $1.228$ times faster carries a proportionally smaller semi-elasticity; $100$\% (no differential); and $122.8$\% (proportional-hazard preservation, which is amplification, not attenuation). In-sample the marginal runs $+7.3$, $+8.9$, $+9.2$ and $+9.6$ points across those four; composed with the off-window calibration, $+4.4$, $+5.4$, $+5.6$ and $+5.8$. That the scaling rises above the committed $0.797\times$ at every positive response is arithmetic, not evidence---a share contributing exactly zero must contribute more at any positive response---and I rest nothing on it. The bracket, not any cell in it, is the result: none of the four is a measured Ginnie marginal, and the overlay-scored $+4.4$ stands unrevised, scoped by the bracket rather than corrected by it.

\paragraph{Danish pipeline mechanisms.}
Two Danish counterfactual mechanisms exist in this framework's pipeline, and only one is live in production. The first is a \emph{rate-gap swap}: holding the baseline hazard, rate-gap coefficient, covariates, and random draw logic fixed, the Danish leg expresses the market-value buyback in rate-equivalent units, so that the buyback discount offsets the locked-in spread. The swap sets $g_{\mathrm{DK}} = g_{\mathrm{US}} + \Delta y_{\mathrm{buyback}} \approx 0$ whenever the loan is out of the money, and $g_{\mathrm{DK}} = g_{\mathrm{US}}$ when rates have fallen and the buyback price is capped at par. At the representative 3.0\% coupon / 6.8\% market state the U.S. gap is $-380$ bp while the Danish effective gap is zero. The U.S. hazard is therefore suppressed by the full locked-in spread while the Danish hazard resets toward its baseline seasoning curve. This swap defines the \emph{mechanism-substitution} variant (the specification behind the superseded 47.1\% Danish CPR of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}), and it is \emph{not} the production mechanism. The production Danish leg is the Berger-recalibrated specification adopted in Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}: it replaces the rate-gap swap with the imported flat moving elasticity, so the production Danish hazard does not respond to the buyback discount at all. The swap machinery remains in the codebase and its PV-versus-par classification is still computed each month, but no production quantity consumes its output, which is why the discount-rate diagnostic of Appendix~\ref{sec:method-abm} moves the Danish leg by exactly \$0.00 billion. Table~\ref{tab:discount} reports that inertness: shifting the buyback discount rate down by 25 to 150 bp (through the primary--secondary spread range) flips thousands of monthly present-value-versus-par classifications while moving the Danish CPR path, roll-off path, and trapped-liquidity dollar by exactly zero on every scoring basis. Two limits apply to that result. I state both because together they retract the immunity I previously claimed from it. First, the diagnostic executed on the Danish-level bracketing leg, not on the production U.S.-intercept leg: its own artifact records a mean Danish CPR of 3.39\%, the bracketing anchor's speed. Under the production anchor the same independence does hold, but by construction, not by measurement, because that branch replaces the rate gap with zeros before the hazard is evaluated. Second, and more important, the result is about the hazard, not about the accounting. The Danish leg credits every retired balance at par: its dynamic-balance loop books roll-off as balance times drain, with no price term anywhere in it. Netting the roughly \$199 billion scheduled component from the leg's \$669.3 billion of Danish roll-off leaves on the order of \$470 billion of buyback-triggered retirement credited at par, against a committed proxy discount grid of 32 to 38\%. A market-value credit would reduce the Danish leg's cash receipts by an amount larger than the $+\$61.2$ billion gap itself, and in the direction that shrinks it. The pipeline does not distinguish a cash haircut from a balance adjustment, and the incidence depends on which the buyback is. So a pre-committed bracket prices both readings from the committed pair (run \texttt{buyback\_credit\_bracket}; gates and the interpretive rule fixed in the script header before execution). Under the balance-adjustment reading face retirement is unchanged and the gap stands at $+\$61.2$ billion. Under the cash-haircut reading receipts fall by the discount on the leg's \$470.7 billion of early face and the gap reverses. The committed grid was asserted, not derived: run \texttt{buyback\_discount\_rederived} prices each month's retired face at its own prepayment-consistent present value---the SOMA book aged to that month at the 2.49\% weighted-average coupon, prepaying at that month's Danish CPR, discounted at that month's market rate---for a face-weighted mean of 23.8\%, a \$112.2 billion haircut, and a cash gap of $-\$51.0$ billion. The price and the face come from two populations (the SOMA book's own vintage tabulation and the microsimulated leg's roll-off): the seam the committed early-face netting already carries, inherited here rather than repaired. That is a point, not a range: the committed 32--38\% span was a range only by virtue of a four-point asserted grid. The monthly discounts do range, from 18.4\% to 28.1\%, and none of the forty-two months reaches the committed grid's floor. The committed grid is consistent with a no-prepayment annuity priced at a representative state: the same primitive returns 35.3\% at zero prepayment on a 3.0\% coupon against a 6.8\% market rate, inside the committed span, where the leg itself prepays at 5.61\% against a window averaging 6.61\%. That identification is plausible rather than exactly reproduced: a band check on the primitive, not a target. The re-derivation shrinks the reversal without retiring it; the threshold it clears is arithmetic on the committed chain, fixed before the run rather than produced by it: the verdict turns at a 13.0\% discount, below every month's priced value. The two readings are not symmetric answers to one question. The benchmark is a face-value object: Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} constructs it from the SOMA current face value series against the par-denominated caps. So the balance-adjustment reading is the benchmark-consistent one, and on the benchmark's own denomination the gap stands. The cash-haircut reading answers a different question, reserves actually drained, and its reversal has a name: the $1-P$ wedge on bought-back face is exactly the par windfall a moving household pays the bondholder under the U.S. rule and keeps under the Danish one, so the cash-flow cost is the disappearance of a household-to-bondholder transfer, not an added resource cost. The gap's sign is therefore incidence-conditional, the channel is priced, and the discount-rate diagnostic still should not be read as immunity to the pricing critique.

\paragraph{The transform's superseded variant and the concave-transform test.}
Two records travel with the transform of \eqref{eq:beta1}. The superseded $0.0693$ is a distinct object: the continuous-hazard variant $-\ln\!\big[\ln(1-(1-\delta)P_q)/\ln(1-P_q)\big] = 0.0693$ (unrounded $0.069258$), which the simulation does not use. The two forms differ by $0.0007$ and both round to $0.069$, but only \eqref{eq:beta1} reproduces the reported results.

The direct test of the log-linear extrapolation is a piecewise-concave transform---full marginal effect over the first 200 bp of lock-in, half beyond. It moves the recovery from 107.0\% to 105.7\% (\$808.4 billion) and slightly improves the contemporaneous path correlation ($+0.281$ versus $+0.190$, peak lag unchanged at $-3$). The log-linear extrapolation is not load-bearing for the level, and is for the marginal---but the asymmetry is one of proportion, not of magnitude, and the same absolute $\pm 1$-point convention cannot support both readings. A pre-committed rerun of the paired legs under the concave transform puts the concave marginal at $+7.87$ points against the production $+9.20$ at the in-sample floor, and at $+5.06$ against $+5.57$ at the off-window point floor. At the in-sample floor the concave null is bit-identical to the production null---\$748.185 billion on both legs, because $\beta_1 = 0$ leaves nothing for the transform to act on---so the level move and the marginal move are numerically the \emph{same} 1.32 points. That 1.32 points is 1.2\% of a 107.0\% recovery and 14\% of a $+9.20$-point marginal. It is that relative reading, not the absolute one, on which the transform is immaterial to the level and material to the marginal. On the absolute $\pm 1$-point materiality threshold the floor-form test of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} applies, and both moves clear it. The transform dimension travels with the marginal's operative uncertainty in Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}.

\paragraph{Sampling schemes for Path B's level and marginal.}
The Liebersohn--Rothstein band of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} is a calibration interval, not sampling error. The sampling dimension is quantified separately, by a stratified loan-level bootstrap specified ex ante in its script header: 200 replicates resampling the 75,000-loan draw with replacement within strata, central and null legs paired per replicate, engine seed held at production. That bootstrap puts the central recovery's 95\% interval at $[107.0, 107.1]\%$ under the standalone scorer (replicate standard deviation 0.03 points), the null's at $[97.8, 97.9]\%$, and the paired lock-in marginal's at $[+9.17, +9.23]$ points (standard deviation 0.016). Re-run under the cluster scheme at this same floor, the paired marginal reads $[+8.27, +10.19]$ points (sd 0.52; run \texttt{bootstrap\_pathb\_cluster}), an understatement of 30.8$\times$ in-window against 37$\times$ off-window. Those intervals are narrow because the scheme holds the design fixed. It resamples loans within each stratum and preserves every stratum's count, so between-stratum composition variance is never drawn. Drawing the 130 three-way draw strata themselves with replacement---at the off-window floor the paper headlines---widens the marginal's interval to $[+4.63, +6.92]$ points with a standard deviation of $0.60$ points (run \texttt{bootstrap\_pathb\_cluster}, 200 replicates). That is a width of $2.29$ points against the within-stratum scheme's $0.062$, so the committed scheme understated loan-sample uncertainty by roughly a factor of $37$. Two properties of that interval are not corrected for and should be stated. Drawing strata with replacement makes the loan count itself random: it runs from 54,134 to 101,404 across replicates against a fixed-design 75,000. So every composition weight is a ratio of random totals, not of fixed ones, and the percentile interval carries no correction for the resulting ratio bias. A fixed-$n$ variant that rescales weights to the design total would settle how much of the width is that bias. It is not run here. The committed point marginal falls inside that interval. Two limits apply to the wider figure. The first is that the strata are very unequal, so the effective cluster count is $25.8$, not $130$ (the largest single stratum is $9.77\%$ of sample balance), and a percentile interval on $26$ effective clusters is itself noisy. The second is that the resampled loan count varies from roughly $54{,}000$ to $101{,}000$. That variation is harmless here only because the engine renormalises the pool to Fed holdings each month, so the level is set exogenously and only composition varies.

\paragraph{Curtailment-layer demonstration runs.} On the accounting identity of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}: a $\pm 25\%$-amplitude seasonal profile and an income-elasticity regime split across 2022--23 and 2024--25 move the netted total to \$70.06 and \$68.09 billion and the wedge to 9.16 and 8.90 points, the marginal unchanged to below $10^{-13}$ points on both legs and the CPR paths bit-identical; a deterministic two-servicer mix (fixed 0.60/0.40 shares, front-loaded and flat group factors) moves the wedge to 9.30 points (\$71.12 billion netted) with the marginal again unchanged, since any servicer-heterogeneous pattern aggregating to one monthly rate is a special case of the same identity.

\section{Path A Estimation Detail and Timing Diagnostics}\label{app:patha-detail}

This appendix collects the estimation detail of Path A (Section~\ref{sec:patha}), the direct input-timing diagnosis, the Danish discount-rate diagnostic, and the monthly-timing exhibits of Section~\ref{sec:identification}.

The second hazard path aggregates Freddie Mac loan-level performance data to the stratum-month level. Strata $s$ are the 296 observed four-way cells of vintage $\times$ coupon $\times$ FICO bucket $\times$ LTV bucket, and the unit of observation is the stratum-month pair $(s,t)$. The estimation configuration sets the bucket edges: FICO below 680, 680--740, and 740 and above; LTV at or below versus above 80; coupons in 0.5-percentage-point steps; vintages 2017--2021. Geography is not a stratum axis, and no geographic term enters \eqref{eq:pathA}. The model is a discrete-time hazard with a log-exposure offset,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pathA}
\log \mathbb{E}\!\left[y_{s,t} \mid x_{s,t}\right] = \log n_{s,t} + \alpha_{s} + f\!\left(a_{s,t}\right) + \beta_g\, g_{s,t} + \beta_b\, b_{s,t} + \beta_\phi\, \phi_t ,
\end{equation}
where $y_{s,t}$ is stratum $s$'s prepaid unpaid principal balance (UPB) in month $t$, in dollars, $n_{s,t}$ its UPB exposure, $x_{s,t}$ the right-hand-side covariates, and $\alpha_{s}$ the stratum fixed effect (295 enter, one absorbed as reference). The term $f(\cdot)$ is a loan-age seasoning spline in mean loan age $a_{s,t}$: a truncated quadratic power basis, a linear term plus $(a - k)_+^2$ at knots $k \in \{12, 24, 36, 60, 84, 120\}$ months, seven basis columns. The remaining terms are $g_{s,t}$, the rate gap in 100 bp units; $b_{s,t}$, the stratum burnout state; and $\phi_t$, the dynamic friction index. The outcome is dollars, not a count. With the log-exposure offset the modeled object is the prepayment rate $y_{s,t}/n_{s,t}$, a nonnegative fraction, so the model is estimated by Poisson pseudo-maximum likelihood, whose score equations remain consistent for the conditional mean without any distributional assumption on $y_{s,t}$; no dispersion statistic is meaningful for this pseudo-likelihood. The 295 stratum fixed effects raise no incidental-parameters problem for the coefficients of interest. Because the model is Poisson, concentrating the fixed effects out of the log-likelihood returns exactly the conditional-likelihood score for the slope vector: the unconditional dummy-variable fit computed here yields the same slope estimates as the conditional estimator, and stays consistent as the number of strata grows with the 36 training months held fixed. The fixed-effect logit or probit does not---its slope estimates are inconsistent at fixed panel length \citep{wooldridge1999}. All that consistency requires is the multiplicative conditional mean $\mathbb{E}[y_{s,t}\mid x_{s,t},\alpha_{s}] = n_{s,t}\exp(\alpha_{s} + x_{s,t}^{\top}\beta)$, with no restriction on the distribution, dispersion, or serial dependence of $y_{s,t}$. All uncertainty statements below come from the two bootstrap schemes of Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap}. The first is a stratum-level cluster bootstrap, robust to arbitrary within-stratum dependence and overdispersion but structurally unable to measure uncertainty in the friction coefficient: $\phi_t$ varies only over time, so resampling strata reproduces the identical friction series in every replication. That burden (and, partially, burnout's) falls to the second, a temporal moving-block bootstrap that resamples the 36 training months in blocks of six with each month's full cross-section intact. Table~\ref{tab:panel} reports the estimation panel's accounting. Both it and Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} are collected in Appendix~\ref{app:ridge}, because Path A is excluded from every headline range by pre-committed specification and neither table enters a headline quantity. The displayed form \eqref{eq:pathA} is the no-seasonal spec v3 design. The production specification (spec v4) adds eleven calendar-month dummies and is the design under which the 121.5\% recovery reported below is computed, though that level is not calendar-attributable (Section~\ref{sec:patha}); every Path A inferential object stays on spec v3. Table~\ref{tab:hazard-notation} collects the notation shared by the two hazard paths.

The 295 stratum fixed effects absorb each cohort's baseline prepayment speed so that the burnout term captures within-pool adverse selection over time. The burnout regressor is a within-stratum demeaned measure of the surviving loan stock, orthogonalized against the loan-age spline so that it is not mechanically collinear with seasoning. This design is the estimation counterpart of \citepos{lesniewski2026} selection identity. The exposure-offset outcome is itself a pool-level, survival-weighted (here UPB-exposure-weighted) hazard. The 295 strata cut the cross-sectional dispersion that drives the identity's negative selection term down to within-cell variation. The demeaned surviving-stock regressor proxies the residual composition drift that the time-invariant fixed effects cannot absorb. An earlier specification using four vintage-year dummies in place of the 295 stratum fixed effects returned a burnout coefficient of the wrong sign (+0.76). Replacing the coarse vintage fixed effects with the full 295-stratum specification and demeaning burnout within stratum removes that wrong-signed artifact. The corrected estimate ($-0.13$) carries the theoretically expected sign: a more heavily burned-out pool should prepay more slowly, holding age and rate gap fixed. But the sign statement is a point-estimate statement only. The temporal bootstrap scheme of Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} centers this coefficient on the opposite sign (mean $+1.23$, median $+0.52$). Under both schemes it is imprecise and not statistically distinguishable from zero. I therefore treat it as a control, not a finding. The fit carries a nominal ridge penalty $\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}}$ on the full coefficient vector, because 295 fixed effects make the model ill-conditioned under plain iteratively reweighted least squares. In 20 strata no training prepayment event is recorded at all, so the likelihood is monotone in their fixed effects and the maximum-likelihood estimates diverge to $-\infty$. I choose $\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}}$ over the grid $\{10^{-5}, 10^{-4}\}$ on a temporally blocked holdout comprising all stratum-months from January 2024 onward, so that no within-stratum information crosses the train/holdout split. The penalty is not what stabilizes the fit. At these magnitudes it is a numerical no-op. The two grid points return coefficient vectors identical to machine precision from the IRLS warm start, so the production coefficients are the IRLS solution and the grid selection is inconsequential. The 20 zero-event strata are held at the finite value the warm-started optimizer leaves them at, and the penalty shrinks them only negligibly. Because the split is temporal over a fixed set of strata, that same fixed effect scores each stratum's post-January-2024 holdout months, so no fixed effect is estimated on, or transferred to, a stratum absent from the training window. The penalty's operative role is as the fallback for resampled refits in the bootstrap machinery. Nor is the production point an artifact of the device. Dropping the 20 zero-event strata and fitting unpenalized Poisson PML reproduces the spec v3 macro coefficients to within $0.002$ in standardized units (rate gap $+0.6727$ against the spec v3 $+0.6734$; under spec v4 the same check holds to within $0.0022$, Appendix~\ref{app:ridge}). Appendix~\ref{app:ridge} records the verification details under both specifications, including a reference-stratum sensitivity of penalized cold-start refits that motivates the warm-started bootstrap below. The battery has been recomputed under spec v4 and the device is unchanged. Two caveats still attach. The same holdout later serves as the out-of-sample diagnostic, so the RMSE reported below is at least weakly favored by selection. Months never touched by any selection step do not exist in this design, one reason Path A is held to a robustness role.
Estimated on this panel, the production specification --- spec v4, the calendar-month seasonal design adopted at the freeze --- yields a rate-gap coefficient of $+0.65$, a burnout coefficient of $-0.17$, and a friction coefficient of $-0.007$, each with the theoretically expected sign. The rate-gap coefficient is standardized: the training standard deviation of the rate gap is 221 bp, so the equivalent per-100 bp coefficient is $+0.29$. The friction coefficient's shrinkage relative to the prior spec ($-0.037$) is expected under the seasonal design, whose eleven month dummies absorb most within-year variation in the time-only friction index. Point estimates alone overstate what is identified here. Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} therefore reports cluster-bootstrap uncertainty for all three, including the bias diagnostics. That uncertainty is estimated under the prior spec v3 (see the table note). The rate-gap point estimate sits near the 3rd percentile of its own bootstrap distribution (bootstrap mean $+1.49$, median $+0.76$, estimated bias $+0.82$). That is a signature of resampling-induced upward skew, under which percentile and basic intervals disagree. The percentile interval excludes zero; the bias-reflecting basic interval does not. I therefore rest no claim on interval-based significance for any Path A coefficient. The defensible statements are about direction and imprecision. The rate-gap coefficient's point estimate is positive, and positive in 197 of 198 converged replications (99.5\%). That share is a property of a right-skewed resampling distribution, not a test. The pre-committed bias-respecting test of $H_0\!: \beta_g \le 0$ does not reject under any construction (BCa $p = 0.093$ stratum / $0.412$ temporal; permutation $p = 0.241$; run \texttt{patha\_sign\_test}). The coefficient is therefore directionally consistent with the imported elasticity without being statistically distinguishable from zero. Its magnitude is identified only up to a wide, construction-dependent interval. Burnout and friction are not distinguishable from zero on any construction. All three enter the production simulation as fitted inputs, none as findings, and no result in this paper depends on their point values. Path A's aggregate recovery now carries the interval that pass produces. I resimulate the QT window under each of the 198 converged replications, swapping in the three macro coefficients per draw and holding intercept, age spline, and fixed effects at the production fit. That yields a median of \$922.6 billion and an interquartile range of \$900.1--\$1{,}130.6 billion, but a 95\% percentile interval of $[-\$4{,}459.8, +\$1{,}190.1]$ billion. A joint rerun swaps in each replication's full coefficient vector: intercept, age spline, and at spec v4 the month effects, drawn together with the macros. That rerun returns $[+\$689.0, +\$1{,}217.6]$ billion, far narrower, and it is not adopted. Of its 198 draws, 106 sit at exactly the zero-voluntary-prepayment floor of \$1{,}217.6 billion, among them all 79 rogue-mode draws --- rogue mode meaning a drawn rate-gap coefficient nearer the reference-swap attractor $+1.96$ than the production $+0.67$. That floor is the trapped figure a pool returns when voluntary prepayment is switched off and only scheduled amortization rolls off, measured by a probe through the same coefficient handoff. The committed interval remains the quoted one, because its $-\$4{,}459.8$ billion tail and the joint rerun's floor pile are the same rogue-mode replicates rendered without and with their compensating intercepts. That is one optimizer pathology of penalized refits on resampled panels, not two sampling statements. Measured at spec v4, the same joint rerun fails 1 of 200 replications against spec v3's 2, and its draws avoid the floor entirely (0 of 199). The seasonal design's instability therefore manifests as rogue-mode attraction (73 of 199 draws), not as fit failure. Fifteen replications with extreme burnout or friction draws (the two coefficients the bootstrap classifies as statistically zero) destabilize the 42-month forward compounding entirely, and 83.8\% of draws land above the benchmark. Those two facts pull in opposite directions, and the unfavorable one belongs in the text as much as the favorable one. The same 198 draws have a \emph{mean} of \$598.9 billion, or 78.3\% of the benchmark --- below the rate-inelastic $\beta_1 = 0$ null's 85.7\% and below Path~B's headline 91.3\% --- against a median of 120.6\%. On the share basis the percentile interval is $[-583.2\%, +155.6\%]$ with a resampling standard error of 201.9 points. I report the mean alongside the median for the same reason the coefficient table does. These distributions are severely right-skewed, so no single summary is sufficient; quoting only the median and the fraction above the benchmark would flatter the estimator. Propagated coefficient uncertainty therefore leaves the forward simulation with no useful precision. That is an additional reason the aggregate dollar figure is held to the aggregate-level role assigned below and is not carried in the abstract. Every Path A inferential object remains estimated under the prior spec v3: Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap}, this interval, and the train/holdout decomposition below. That is because bootstrap replications under the spec v4 seasonal design are branch-unstable. The resampled penalized likelihood has a second optimum that attracts replications away from the production branch. On the production panel itself 1 of 50 dispersed starts lands on the production branch, and no start reaches a better penalized objective (run \texttt{pathA\_cold\_starts}). The objective evaluated at the frozen coefficient vector is the best value any of 150 starts attains, and every non-production terminus sits at least seven orders of magnitude above it. That is an optimizer stall of a strictly convex objective. Until the resampled instability is stabilized, the production specification carries no valid uncertainty of its own. Path A is excluded from the paper's headline figures for this reason, retained as an aggregate-context, non-corroborating exhibit. Simulating forward, this model recovers \$928.9 billion, or 121.5\%, of the \$764.7 billion empirical benchmark under the production calendar-month specification (spec v4). The forward simulation drains each cohort's balance deterministically each month by balance times hazard, with fractional prepayment settling directly to simulated SOMA roll-off and no settlement-lag convolution. That specification was adopted at the pre-submission freeze under pre-committed parity gates that reproduce the committed seasonal robustness artifact exactly. The prior specification is preserved as a spec-v3 exhibit.

Three diagnostics temper this aggregate result, because they bear directly on how much weight the \$928.9 billion figure can carry. First, the holdout fit, evaluated under spec v3, is weak in both weightings. Re-scoring the frozen spec v4 fit on the same January-2024-onward holdout, without refitting, gives 37.9 unweighted and 3.0 exposure-weighted percentage points against spec v3's 37.8 and 2.5, so the seasonal terms do not improve the out-of-sample fit. The units matter here. The unweighted RMSE of approximately 38 percentage points of annualized stratum-month CPR is dominated by small-exposure strata with unstable realized rates, while the exposure-weighted RMSE, the weighting the fit itself uses, is 2.5 percentage points against an empirical mean CPR of 5.14\% (ABM basis), half the mean. Neither weighting supports out-of-sample predictive power at the stratum-month level, and the ridge grid's two candidate fits are numerically identical on this holdout, so no selection hinges on the weighting. A flexible-learner ceiling test bounds how much of this weakness is functional form. A gradient-boosted Poisson learner given the same covariate information on the identical pre-committed holdout improves the exposure-weighted RMSE materially, to 0.9 points against the frozen spec v4's 3.0. It leaves the unweighted RMSE essentially unmoved (37.6 against 37.9) and out-of-sample $R^2$ at zero ($+0.003$). A penalized Poisson GLM on the exact v4 design does no better than the production fit (2.6 and 38.1 points). Read against the run's pre-committed thresholds, flexibility buys recalibration of the high-exposure cells but no cross-cell predictive power in the 2024-onward regime. The landmarking-with-recalibration strand receives the same test. An exposure-weighted isotonic map fit on the 2023 inner-validation year only, applied to the frozen spec v4 holdout predictions, improves the exposure-weighted RMSE to 2.53 points against the frozen 3.04, short of the pre-committed 0.80$\times$ threshold. It leaves the unweighted RMSE at 37.8 and out-of-sample $R^2$ at zero ($-0.007$). The pre-committed verdict is no material change: a monotone level recalibration fit in the training regime does not transfer predictive power across the 2024 boundary. Second, the recovery is concentrated where the model is unsupervised. The decomposition is measured under spec v3 and was not recomputed at adoption. Of the spec v3 fit's \$915.1 billion, \$331.0 billion accrues in the 19 QT months overlapping the training window (June 2022--December 2023), against \$337.5 billion empirical (98\%). A further \$584.1 billion accrues in the 23 post-training simulation months from January 2024 onward, against \$427.3 billion empirical (137\%); 21 of those months have performance data, and the empirical comparison uses those. Components are independently rounded, and the overshoot lives almost entirely out of sample. Third, the raw CPR correlation against the empirical path is $r = -0.444$ at zero lag, more negative even than the ABM's $-0.318$, and \eqref{eq:pathA} omits calendar-month seasonality, one of the four factors of \citepos{richard1989} canonical prepayment function and a natural first suspect for a monthly path fit this poor. Re-estimating with eleven calendar-month dummies confirms strong, correctly signed seasonality (spring and early-fall log-effects up to $+0.43$ against a January base) and improves the simulated path's contemporaneous correlation from $-0.444$ to $-0.378$, with the peak moving from lag $-3$ to $-2$. That timing improvement is calendar-attributable, and I test it. Permuting the assignment of the twelve estimated month log-effects to calendar months preserves their multiset exactly, and therefore every level channel they drive, while destroying only their alignment with calendar time. I score the permutation on the conjunctive criterion the claim actually states: a lag-0 correlation better than $-0.378$ \emph{and} a peak at lag $-2$. Scrambled assignments win 1 of 64 draws, a one-sided $p$ of 0.031. An independent sixteen-draw family at a different seed also returns a single win (\texttt{pathA\_seasonal\_permutation}). The two components scored separately, 11 and 8 of 64, are diagnostics only: each necessarily exceeds the joint count, so reading either alone understates a conjunctive claim. The recovery figure is a different matter, and I no longer credit it to the calendar terms. Across those same 64 scrambles the recovery spans 121.14--122.29\%, a range of 1.15 points that contains the adopted 121.5\%, so the level is calendar-invariant: permutation holds the multiset of month effects fixed, and it is the multiset, not its alignment, that sets the level. Nor is the spec-v4 total decomposable as production plus seasonality in the first place. The seasonal refit with all twelve month multipliers zeroed moves the recovery by $+\$80.27$ billion on its own, and restoring the multipliers claws back $-\$66.44$ billion of that, against a net reported seasonal effect of $+\$13.83$ billion. The timing improvement is real but far from sufficient: the named first suspect accounts for only a modest share of the negative path fit. A model that recovers 121.5\% of the dollar benchmark while mismatching the monthly path this badly may be recovering the aggregate partly through offsetting errors, over-predicting prepayment in some cohort-months and under-predicting in others in a way that nets out near the benchmark without the underlying mechanism being correctly timed. I therefore treat Path A's recovery as evidence about the aggregate level only. No timing claim rests on Path B either: its apparent levels advantage does not survive detrending (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), and every claim this paper makes for Path B is a level-accuracy claim. Table~\ref{tab:pathadiag} collects Path A's diagnostics in one place.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{fig2_ccf}
\caption{Cross-correlation functions between each estimator's simulated CPR path and the empirical SOMA-derived path, lags $-6$ to $+6$. Under the implemented convention (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), a peak at negative lag means the empirical series leads and the simulated path trails. Path B's $-3$/$-4$ peak and Path A's and the ABM's negative contemporaneous correlations summarize the same fact from Table~\ref{tab:estimators}: no estimator shows positive contemporaneous alignment, and once the window's shared trend is removed no estimator's co-movement is distinguishable from zero under the moving-block bands.}
\label{fig:ccf}
\end{figure}

A moving-block bootstrap (block length 6, 4,000 replications) puts the 95\% interval on the peak-lag correlation at $[-0.21, +0.49]$ and on the lag-0 correlation at $[-0.13, +0.44]$. With seven lags searched, individual band-point differences in peak $r$ carry no evidential weight. These intervals are not sensitive to the block-length choice in any verdict-relevant way: at block lengths 4 and 8 the peak-lag endpoints move to $[-0.27, +0.39]$ and $[-0.22, +0.51]$, and the lag-0 endpoints to $[-0.11, +0.45]$ and $[-0.17, +0.42]$, every interval still containing zero.

A Theil decomposition formalizes where each estimator's monthly error lives. It is a fit diagnostic, not a timing credential. On levels, the ABM's mean-squared error is 68\% bias share (the level failure already visible in its aggregate). Path B's is 11.3\% bias and 82.5\% variance share on the corrected amortization basis (run \texttt{coupon\_convention\_amortization}). Its residual monthly error is under-dispersion against a seasonal empirical path, not level bias. Path A is intermediate (26.5\% bias, 63.0\% covariance share). After linear detrending the variance share still dominates for Path B (93.7\%). On first differences, Theil's $U_2$ is 1.23 for the ABM, 1.01 for Path A, and 1.00 for Path B. No estimator beats a naive no-change forecast of month-over-month CPR changes, which restates the timing concession above in forecast-accuracy units. Table~\ref{tab:theil} collects the full decomposition by transform, with cumulative dollar-error profiles whose terminal values reproduce Table~\ref{tab:estimators}'s aggregates by construction.

A direct input-timing diagnosis rules out the dating of the rate input and narrows, but does not isolate, the mechanism. In monthly \emph{changes}, the simulated CPR responds to the contemporaneous rate change with the correct sign and near-mechanical strength ($\Delta$-correlation $-0.94$ at lag 0). The empirical CPR's month-to-month changes show no detectable rate response ($+0.25$, inside the $\pm 0.31$ zero band at $n = 41$). At monthly frequency the empirical path's variation is dominated by non-rate factors the simulation does not model, calendar seasonality above all. One construction asymmetry belongs in this reading as well. The empirical CPR back-out retains the income-scaled curtailment component (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}), while the simulated CPR paths exclude it, since curtailment enters only as a common dollar flow in the accounting layer. The two series being correlated therefore differ by a small, slowly-varying component as well as by seasonality.

The decomposition also carries its time axis. The monthly marginal path is the central-minus-null difference of the two committed simulated legs, and its sum reproduces the $+\$70.3$ billion bit-exactly. That path is positive in all 42 window months, splits 21.3\%/41.9\%/36.8\% across the window's thirds, and peaks at \$2.29 billion in November 2023, the window's eighteenth month. By coupon bucket the terminal marginal divides 38.0\%/43.7\%/18.3\% across the $<3.0\%$/3.0--4.0\%/$\geq 4.0\%$ groups on 40.3\%/40.4\%/19.3\% of sample balance.
