\section{Path A Estimation Panel, Coefficient Uncertainty, and Ridge Device}\label{app:ridge}

The replication package verifies three facts about the ridge device of Section~\ref{sec:patha}, under both specifications: the spec v3 records stand, each fact has been recomputed under spec v4, and the device is unchanged. First, at production magnitudes the penalty is a numerical no-op on the full panel. The two grid points ($\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}} \in \{10^{-5}, 10^{-4}\}$) return coefficient vectors identical to machine precision from the IRLS warm start (maximum parameter difference $4.5\times10^{-17}$ in the replication artifact; $8.4\times10^{-17}$ under spec v4). Grid selection is therefore inconsequential, and the production coefficients are the IRLS solution to machine precision; ``mildly shrunk'' would overstate an effect that is nil. Second, ridge on the full coefficient vector with one stratum absorbed as reference is not invariant to the reference choice. In cold-start refits on resampled panels, swapping the reference stratum can move the standardized rate-gap coefficient by more than its own magnitude --- an optimizer-path artifact of penalized refits with the 20 inestimable fixed effects. The bootstrap machinery accordingly warm-starts every replication and discards non-converged draws. The warm-started spec v4 swap refit is reference-invariant to $7.3\times10^{-14}$, consistent with the warm-start policy that concession motivated. That invariance is not evidence that the cold-start sensitivity is gone. Third, the production point itself is not an artifact of these choices. A refit that drops the 20 zero-event strata (423 of 10,176 cells) and fits unpenalized Poisson PML converges, is exactly invariant to the reference choice, and reproduces the spec v3 macro coefficients to within $0.002$ in standardized units: rate gap is $+0.6727$ against the spec v3 $+0.6734$, and friction $-0.0373$ against $-0.0371$, third-decimal agreement. Burnout is $-0.1317$ against $-0.1301$; the larger burnout gap reflects that dropping the 423 cells changes the burnout regressor's within-stratum demeaning and age-orthogonalization sample. At spec v4 the identical 20 strata and 423 cells drop out, since the month dummies do not change the zero-event set. The same refit is again exactly reference-invariant ($1.1\times10^{-14}$) and reproduces the spec v4 macro coefficients to within $0.0022$: rate gap $+0.6463$ against $+0.6469$, friction $-0.00715$ against $-0.00711$, burnout $-0.1734$ against $-0.1713$, against a tolerance pre-committed at $0.005$, loosened from v3's $0.002$ because the month dummies absorb time variation the friction coefficient formerly carried.

Table~\ref{tab:panel} reports the estimation panel this fit is built on, and Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} the coefficient uncertainty the two bootstrap schemes of Section~\ref{sec:patha} return. Both sit here because Path A is an aggregate-context, non-corroborating exhibit that pre-committed specification excludes from every headline range; no cell of either table enters a headline quantity.

\begin{tablenotes}[flushleft]\footnotesize
\item Stratum-cluster scheme: the observed vintage--coupon--FICO--LTV strata are resampled with replacement, each resampled stratum receives a fresh fixed-effect label so duplicated strata contribute independent fixed effects, and per-replication standardization (including the burnout orthogonalization, which is refit inside every replication) is rescaled to production standardized units. This scheme is structurally silent about the friction coefficient: the friction index varies only over time, so every replication sees the identical series, and the stratum-scheme friction row understates that coefficient's uncertainty by construction. Temporal scheme: the 36 training months are resampled in moving blocks of six with each month's cross-section kept intact, same refit and rescaling; it is the informative scheme for friction and, partially, burnout; its friction SE (2.80) is roughly $5.5\times$ the stratum scheme's (0.51). The ridge penalty is held at the production $\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}} = 10^{-4}$ rather than re-selected per replication (re-selection would bootstrap a different, coefficient-plus-model-selection estimator). Replications returning non-finite or implausible ($|\beta| > 20$) coefficients are counted as failures, not draws; the point refit reproduces the production coefficients to four decimals on the same 10,176 training cells. Coefficients are in production standardized units (training SD of the rate gap: 221 bp). The stratum-scheme burnout median coincides with the point estimate at reported precision only (unrounded: median $-0.1297$, point $-0.1301$). Mean, median, and the implied bias (mean $-$ point) are reported because both schemes' rate-gap distributions are heavily right-skewed; under that skew the percentile and basic 95\% intervals disagree about zero, so interval-based significance is construction-dependent and the text relies on sign stability only; but the bootstrap sign-share is itself percentile logic, and the pre-committed bias-respecting test of $H_0\!: \beta_g \le 0$ (run \texttt{patha\_sign\_test}; BCa with the 296-refit jackknife acceleration this note previously deferred, plus a month-permutation leg) does not reject under any construction ($p = 0.093$/$0.412$/$0.241$), so the 99.5\% positive share is a description of a right-skewed resampling distribution, not a sign test, and no sign claim for this coefficient survives.
\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

\section{Dynamic-Fit Decomposition}\label{app:theil}

Table~\ref{tab:theil} collects the Theil fit diagnostics summarized in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}: $U$ statistics, the bias/variance/covariance decomposition of each estimator's mean-squared error on three transforms of the monthly CPR paths, and the terminal cumulative dollar errors. The $\beta_1 = 0$ null's cumulative-error profile follows from the committed legs by arithmetic. Its terminal cumulative error against the empirical path is $-\$16.6$ billion, versus Path B's $+\$53.8$ billion: the null under-runs the empirical roll-off by approximately the lock-in marginal, by construction. The full monthly profile is committed with the marginal-timing data of Section~\ref{sec:identification}.

Grouped calibration localizes the same verdict (run \texttt{grouped\_calibration}). I bin the frozen Path A cell predictions into five exposure-weighted prediction-CPR quintiles, with edges fit on the training window only, and cross them with calendar half-years. The training halves calibrate at a pooled predicted-to-realized ratio of 1.000, exact by construction of the exposure-offset fit, with per-half ratios spanning 0.80--1.17. The post-2024 holdout instead drifts into monotonically worsening under-prediction: 0.64, 0.57, 0.44, 0.43 across 2024H1--2025H2, pooled predicted 2.24 against realized 4.21 points. The departure is temporal drift, not a reliability-slope failure: pooled across periods the four upper prediction bins calibrate at ratios 0.94--1.11, and the shortfall concentrates in the lowest-prediction bin (43.5\% of exposure), where the 2024--25 cells accumulate. That is the finer-resolution form of the $U_2 > 1$ diagnosis above. This is a cell-panel CPR object, distinct from the cumulative dollar-runoff split of Section~\ref{sec:patha}, and like every Path A diagnostic it moves no headline figure.

\section{Composition Shift: Estimation Sample versus SOMA Book}\label{app:composition}

Table~\ref{tab:composition} reports a formal covariate-shift analysis between the Freddie Mac data behind the hazard paths and the SOMA book the benchmark spans, on the dimensions both sides carry. The book side is the New York Fed's CUSIP-level SOMA holdings, parsed by the same production code that builds the ABM cohorts and anchor-gated to the committed marginals (book WAC 2.49\%, Ginnie share 20.4\%, term split, vintage-group shares). The API also served the historical June 29, 2022 as-of, whose composition is consistent with those anchors; the artifact reports that as-of as a non-gated secondary block. The shifts on coupon, vintage, and agency are large --- the expected result, disclosed in prose throughout the paper --- and each carries a committed, signed bound as its consequence, listed in the table's final column. The joint agency$\times$vintage cells settle the one netting question the marginals leave open: of the 2022 vintage's 23.1\% of book face, 20.7\% is conventional and 2.4\% is the Ginnie intersection, so vintage and agency marginals cannot be added without double-counting that cell. Geography, loan size, FICO, and LTV do not exist at the CUSIP level of the Fed's published holdings, which carry par value and security description only. Panel (b) supplies the deliverable substitute: the Freddie and Fannie loan-level samples agree to negligible shift on exactly those dimensions. The hazard specification's exposure to the loan-level covariates is bracketed directly by the printed covariate-block endpoints (99.2\%/92.2\% shared-basis, Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).

\section{Floor-Sweep and Contamination Mechanics}\label{app:floormech}

This appendix collects the mechanics behind the calibration box of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}: the floor-level sweep, the marginal as a curve in the imported elasticity, the separation of the two refinance contaminations behind the off-window floor anchors, the out-of-sample anchor construction, the calibration catalogue for the headline marginal row, and the inference ladder on the floor read (Table~\ref{tab:ladder}). The three exhibits themselves are read where the headline is set, in Section~\ref{sec:hazard} (Tables~\ref{tab:floorband}, \ref{tab:lowband}, and~\ref{tab:oosfloor}).

Table~\ref{tab:floorband} reports the lock-in marginal at every cell of the resulting calibration box. Three facts shape the reading. First, the pre-committed stability threshold ($\pm 2$ points across the empirically plausible 3--5\% floor range) fails: at the central elasticity the marginal spans $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ points across that range. The mechanism is structural. The hazard specification takes a maximum against the floor, so wherever the floor binds the elasticity is inert, and raising the floor mechanically crowds out the lock-in channel. Second, both pre-committed monotonicities hold: the marginal decreases in the floor within every band row and increases in the band at every floor. The box maximum lands at the predicted corner, $+13.2$ points ($+\$100.7$ billion) at a 2\% floor on the 7.7\% band edge. Third, every cell is positive, the minimum $+2.1$ points at a 6\% floor on the 5.5\% band edge, and every marginal in the box is basis-invariant, because the shared-accounting netting is common to the central and null legs of each cell (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}). That positivity is a consistency check on the specification, not an identifying result, since the window's rate configuration fixes the sign before any floor or band is chosen (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). The verification reading of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} accordingly weakens from a point to a bounded interval. The lock-in elasticity's identified contribution is $+9.2$ points at the production calibration, $+5.0$ to $+12.6$ points across the empirically plausible floor range, and $+2.1$ to $+13.2$ points across the full box. The off-window re-measurement below places the headline reading at $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points. The aggregate level remains floor-dominated throughout.

The box sweeps the elasticity only over its published band, and that band's lower edge is a literature boundary, not an evidential one. The in-sample estimates cannot reject zero for the elasticity (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), so a reader who weighs that non-rejection over the imported band needs the marginal at values the box never visits. A pre-committed extension (run \texttt{band\_low\_extension}) re-runs the paired legs at quarterly-decline values $\delta \in \{0, 1.0, 2.0, 3.0, 3.25, 4.0, 5.0\}\%$ below the band at both anchor floors; its grid, its parity gates against the committed production and off-window cells, and its monotonicity check were fixed in the script header before any run. Table~\ref{tab:lowband} reports the resulting curve beside the band's own cells. The curve is monotone at both floors, passes through zero at $\delta = 0$ by construction, and is concave at the off-window floor through the censoring mechanism of Section~\ref{sec:identification}: raising the response crowds the hazard into the floor (the bind share climbs from 35.8\% to 73.5\% along that curve), so each increment of elasticity buys less marginal. At half the adopted central, $\delta = 3.25\%$, the marginal is $+5.1$ points ($+\$39.1$ billion) at the in-sample floor and $+3.5$ points ($+\$26.5$ billion) at the headline off-window floor. This curve prices the dependence on the imported elasticity rather than identifying it: how small the marginal could be, if the moving response is weaker than the literature band, is read off the curve, and the identified content remains the bounded range stated in Section~\ref{sec:identification}.


Those two checks vary the floor's level and its functional form. A third pre-committed check interrogates the level's provenance: whether the 4\% figure is an artifact of being measured in-window on the locked-in cohorts the marginal later decomposes. Two runs carry it, \texttt{out\_of\_window\_floor} and \texttt{oos\_identification}, and spec, anchor definitions, acceptance thresholds, and parity gate were fixed in each script header before its runs, the ex-ante convention the replication package's ledger records. Run in-window on the deeply out-of-the-money 2023--24 discount cohorts, the identical exposure-weighted-CPR procedure reproduces the production floor at 3.8--3.9\% annual CPR, flat across seasoning cuts and validating the measurement where it is used. Applied out-of-window to pooled 2017--2019 discount-cohort turnover, before both the QT window and the 2020--21 refinancing wave, it recovers a materially higher 6.1\%. That pooled reading, however, does not survive its own diagnostics, and I do not take it as the operative off-window anchor.

Two contaminations are separable, and separating them is what changes the reading. The first is seasoning. A floor meant to capture strictly involuntary turnover---death, divorce, relocation---should be approximately flat in loan age, but the pooled 2017--2019 out-of-the-money turnover \emph{rises} monotonically with it: 3.68\% below twelve months, 5.78\% at twelve to twenty-four, and 10.99\% at twenty-four to thirty-six. The second is rate regime. Splitting the pooled window at the 2018/2019 turn separates a rising-rate leg (the 30-year rate moved 4.03\% to 4.64\% over 2018, suppressing refinancing incentive) from a falling-rate leg (4.46\% to 3.72\% over 2019, a refinancing wave). The legs read very differently: the 2018 leg returns 4.70--5.33\% across the out-of-the-money gap grid at the age-$\geq$-12 cut, while the 2019 leg returns 6.91\% and carries the entire 10.99\% age-$\geq$-24 cell. The pooled 6.1\% is therefore a refinancing-inflated composite, not a baseline turnover read, and the 2019 leg is disqualified on both diagnostics at once. The rate-regime half of that disqualification, as stated, compares the two legs at different discount depths; the matched-depth reconciliation below repairs the comparison and reports what survives it, which is less than the sentence just written implies.

What remains is the 2018 rising-rate leg, and its status must be stated precisely, because the paper's off-window anchor now rests on it. Its mature-age seasoning test is \emph{not computable}: the leg contains no out-of-the-money cohort-months at all past the twenty-four-month age cut, because a loan that is out of the money while rates are rising is necessarily a recent, low-coupon origination and therefore young. The 2018 leg is thus clean by rate-regime construction, not by test, and its one computable age contrast (3.78\% to 5.33\% across the twelve-month cut) is confounded by the PSA ramp, on which young loans genuinely turn over less, so it is not evidence about the contrast the leg is missing. The out-of-agency read is that in every cell where a mature contrast exists at all, mature turnover is the higher one, and Section~\ref{sec:identification} reports by how much. A second limitation concerns depth. The gap-$\leq$-two-point cell at which the in-window anchor is measured does exist off-window: the 2018 leg's gap runs to $-2.87$ points, and it holds 35 cohort-months reading 3.440\% annual CPR. Grids that stop at half a point do so because their thresholds were fixed at zero, a quarter point, and half a point in the script header, not because the rate history forbade anything deeper. The true limitation is exposure collapse, and a matched-depth run (\texttt{matched\_depth\_reconciliation}) measures it: down the 2018 leg's depth ladder the share of its gap-$\leq$-0 exposure that survives runs 100\%, 83.7\%, 72.9\%, 35.9\%, 11.3\%, 3.9\%, and 0.02\%, so the two-point cell carries \$0.282 billion of \$1{,}264.5 billion. That cell is present and worthless, and an ex-ante support rule excludes it before any reading is taken: a depth is admissible for a leg only if the leg retains at least 10\% of its gap-$\leq$-0 exposure there. These are the reasons the off-window floor resolves to a \emph{range}, and I report it as one.


\paragraph{Calibration entries for the headline marginal row.} Fannie Mae read of the same selection rule on the pooled off-window period 5.52\%, above the clean band and bracketing the marginal below $+4.3$, its like-for-like Freddie counterpart 5.185\% rather than the 2018 leg's 4.991\%, so 0.337 points of that distance is agency and the rest is period; $+3.9$ to $+7.5$ joint with the band; additive form $+11.2$ (form-conditional hull $+3.5$ to $+13.1$); concave transform $+5.1$; the two \emph{jointly} $+9.2$, an interaction of $-1.47$ points, so these two entries do not add; Ginnie overlay $+4.4$ (cuts the same way as the age-standardized read; now run jointly with it in run \texttt{b5\_joint\_cell}: composed $+2.9$, within $0.1$ of proportional); floor-stability check $+\$45.1$B; baseline-level sweep 75--150 PSA $+0.9$ to $+15.6$ (a convention range, no coverage property; run \texttt{psa\_level\_sweep}); covariate endpoints move this marginal by only $+0.1$ while spanning 8.2 points of level; survival-selection attenuation $+5.1$/$+4.6$/$+3.7$ at $\eta = 0.86/0.73/0.54$, the frailty-identity mapping $\eta = S_{\mathrm{pre}}^{\xi}$ at the measured $S_{\mathrm{pre}} = 40{,}234/75{,}000$ and $\xi = 0.25/0.5/1$ (run \texttt{attenuation\_sensitivity}; a transport sensitivity on the imported coefficient, listed in Table~\ref{tab:assembly} as a bracket in $\xi$ rather than as a correction).

\paragraph{The inference ladder on the floor read.} Section~\ref{sec:identification} quotes the restricted wild-cluster inversion rung of Table~\ref{tab:ladder} as the binding layer; the full ladder, with its degrees of freedom, follows.

\noindent{\footnotesize \emph{Notes to Table~\ref{tab:ladder}.} The Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom are data-driven and estimator-specific, so one value cannot serve all three cluster-robust variants: the 6.2 and 5.1 shown are CR1's and CR2's, and CR3's own is smaller still. CR1 applies no leverage adjustment --- it is the narrowest of the three sandwiches --- and is the standard error the wild rows studentize with, so its two rows are the ladder's baseline rather than competing reads. Its Bell--McCaffrey row printing CR3's conventional-df interval is an accident of this leverage profile, not an identity. Unrounded, CR1 at Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom runs $[+2.753, +8.796]$ and CR3 at conventional degrees of freedom $[+2.773, +8.774]$ --- two distinct intervals that agree only once each endpoint is taken to one decimal. The effective cluster count is 5.9 by the same Herfindahl-inverse convention as the loan/stratum scheme's 25.8, against 31 nominal clusters. The run's recorded maximum single-cluster leverage is 0.33, in the 2017-vintage, 4.0\% coupon, FICO $740+$, LTV ${\leq}80$ stratum. That is ten times an equal 31-cluster share and 1.9 times the next largest (the same cohort at LTV ${>}80$, 0.17). That concentration is why the wild-cluster correction is applied and the percentile read demoted. It does not rank the wild construction against the Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom, and I do not claim it does. The wild rows re-draw cluster-level weights at that same leverage profile; the Bell--McCaffrey rows keep the sandwich and correct the reference distribution's degrees of freedom for it. At 5.9 effective clusters neither correction dominates. So the primary read is now adjudicated, not asserted. Run \texttt{fewcluster\_coverage}'s pre-committed rule --- narrowest construction with Gaussian coverage $\geq 93.0\%$ and heavy-tail coverage $\geq 91.0\%$ --- selects the restricted inversion, the artifact's own \texttt{binding\_construction} field, whose $[+2.3, +9.1]$ this paper quotes as the binding layer. The Webb wild-$t$, at 90.8\%/91.4\%, falls below that bar, and its $[+2.9, +8.7]$ is retained as a reported rung, not the quoted interval. The run's own \texttt{landing\_branch} string records L1 (Webb retains), which contradicts the rule its spec froze. The string is sha-pinned and left unedited, and this note is the adjudication. CR2 at Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom ($+2.4$ to $+9.1$ points), the other qualifying rung, stands beside the restricted inversion, over-covering and wider. The three rungs printed wider still understate their own width. CR3 at Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom, the restricted inversion and the two-way rung have \emph{lower} endpoints at floors above the committed grid's 6.0\% end and are censored to it, and the marginal falls as the floor rises, so all three run below the $+2.3$ printed. Their upper endpoints are interior. The last two rungs re-cluster the same read rather than re-estimating it: the month rung groups the 137 cohort-months by the six calendar months the 2018 leg populates, July to December, and the two-way rung adds the CR1 variance clustered on the 31 strata to the one clustered on those six months and subtracts the one clustered on their 137 intersection cells, the Cameron--Gelbach--Miller construction, carried at $\min(G)-1 = 5$ degrees of freedom. Both come in wider than the CR1 rung they re-cluster, so the month-level shock is priced here rather than dismissed. They widen for different reasons, and only the two-way rung widens because its variance is larger. Month clustering widens the ladder, as I pre-committed it would and for the reason the specification gave, the drop from 31 clusters to six: CR1 goes from 5.20 to 5.33 points of width, both unrounded. Its variance runs the other way, which that specification allowed. The month-clustered CR1 standard error comes in 18\% \emph{smaller} than the stratum-clustered one, so at this cluster unit the common shock adds no variance, and the widening is the six-cluster reference distribution alone. Clustering on both margins at once does add variance---the Cameron--Gelbach--Miller standard error runs 16\% \emph{above} the stratum-clustered one---and that rung is the wider of the two new rows; whether it would be computable at all I deliberately did not predict. My second pre-commitment missed. I expected the month rung's lower marginal edge to be censored at the grid's 6.0\% end, as the two widest stratum rungs already are, and it is not---both endpoints of the printed rung are interior, and of the two new rows only the two-way one censors. Among the month rung's own variants only the unprinted Rademacher one reaches past that edge. Six clusters is small in more than one direction. The month rung's own Bell--McCaffrey degrees of freedom is 3.9---below the four my pre-committed too-few-clusters rule was written to protect, though that rule's own $G < 5$ trigger did not fire. Six clusters admit only $2^{6} = 64$ distinct Rademacher sign vectors, so a Rademacher wild-$t$ tail probability at this cluster unit cannot resolve below $1/64$, which is why the Webb six-point weights are primary here, as they are above.\par}

\section{Behavioral Extension Mechanics}\label{sec:robustness-extensions}

This appendix gives the mechanism behind each of the three behavioral extensions summarized in Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}. As Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions} describes, loss aversion steepens the penalty every household faces. On its own that would suppress simulated mobility below the historically observed 4--5\% baseline turnover floor, so to preserve the floor I re-calibrate the mobility-desire parameter upward, nearly tripling it. That recalibration mechanically inflates the model's overall responsiveness to rate gaps, so the jump to 54.9\% is an artifact of holding the turnover floor fixed, not a validation of loss aversion as a mechanism. Because that floor is anchored at maximum lock-in, the sign of the recalibration's effect on rate-sensitivity is not fixed. A specification change that eases turnover at the anchor lowers the recalibrated mobility scale and deflates rate-sensitivity: in the real-covariate cross-design of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} the scale falls to 36,086 from the synthetic 43,883. The headline recovery, itself resting on a floor-anchored calibration, inherits that indeterminacy. That is why the ABM's negative verdict is read only alongside the cross-design hedge and never on its own. The second extension, income-scaled curtailment, pulls the estimate down to 45.1\%. Curtailment is a strictly additive drag on prepayment, so this decline is what I predicted ex ante from a diagnostic correlation between the ABM's over-prediction and disposable-income growth (Appendix~\ref{sec:method-frictions}). The third extension, multi-vintage coupon composition, pulls the estimate down further, to 33.7\%. A lower weighted-average coupon widens the gap to prevailing market rates, and my hypothesis was that this should increase predicted trapped liquidity, not reduce it. The inversion suggests the ABM's CPR mechanism is not translating a wider coupon gap into slower prepayment the way the household-choice framework predicts it should. I have not diagnosed this inversion, and its implication cuts in both directions. An aggregate response to the rate gap running backward is equally consistent with ``household choice explains little'' and with ``this specific ABM's gap response is mis-specified.'' So the ABM's recovery share is never quoted in this paper without the cross-design qualifier of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}, and the paradigm attribution inherits that section's hedge. It is also why the two-estimator contrast, not the ABM's point estimate, is what the paper asks the reader to carry. I cannot rule out that a differently specified household model would perform better, but that possibility does not bear on the results reported in Section~\ref{sec:abm}.

The wait-and-see freeze now carries a direct sensitivity run, scored on the frozen Danish-anchor production accounting (trapped \$84.5 billion at the production 150 bp trigger and 20\% share; parity gate $+\$0.02$ billion). Removing the freeze entirely drops trapped liquidity to \$44.6 billion, an isolated contribution of $+\$39.9$ billion, or $+5.2$ points of benchmark. Moving the six-month trigger to 100 or 200 bp spans \$105.9 to \$70.5 billion, so the trigger's placement matters at the few-points scale, and its author-calibrated status remains a disclosed limitation. Sweeping the freeze share with the floor anchor retained gives \$65.1 billion at a 10\% share, \$81.5 billion at the externally implied 18.3\%, and \$103.3 billion at 30\%. Re-anchoring the share to the Liebersohn--Rothstein-implied value moves recovery by only $-0.4$ points of benchmark. The calibrated mobility scale and floor CPR are bit-identical at every share, confirming that the freeze cannot compromise the baseline turnover floor. The front-end DTI gate gets the parallel treatment. Shifting the 43\% wall to 36\% or 50\% on the same frozen surface moves ABM trapped liquidity to \$153.3 and \$119.7 billion, from \$84.5 billion at the production 43\%. The turnover floor is re-anchored into the empirical 4--5\% band at every threshold. The gate is therefore material to the ABM's own estimate while never breaching the floor. That sweep is non-monotone in the wall, and the production 43\% sits at its minimum, which invites the reading that the paper's own parameter choice is the one minimizing its own estimate. That reading does not survive decomposition (run \texttt{dti\_nonmonotonicity\_decomposition}). The floor-retention rule re-calibrates the mobility-desire scale only where the fixed-scale floor would leave the 4--5\% band, and across these three thresholds that happens exactly once: at 50\%, where loosening the wall lifts the fixed-scale floor to 5.09\% and the search cuts the scale from 43{,}883 to 41{,}934. Holding the scale at its production value instead, the sweep is monotone (\$153.4, \$84.6 and \$46.4 billion at 36, 43 and 50\%), and the 50\% leg's entire rebound is the re-calibration, worth $-\$73.4$ billion. The frozen and re-calibrated legs are bit-identical at 36 and 43\%, where the rule is a no-op, which is what identifies the channel. The wall is therefore monotone in the direction the mechanism implies, and the published non-monotonicity is the free parameter absorbing the floor constraint. Nothing here moves a headline: the ABM is a stress-test device and the hazard marginal carries no DTI gate.

\section{Duration Quantification: Approximate Portfolio Weighted-Average Life}\label{sec:robustness-wal}

The mechanism the paper quantifies is duration extension, so I express it in maturity units. Table~\ref{tab:wal} is printed with the cap-design arithmetic in Section~\ref{sec:discussion-capdesign}. It reports the SOMA book's approximate weighted-average life (WAL) under scheduled amortization only, under the empirical CPR path, and under each estimator's implied path, at the QT window's start and end. This appendix documents its construction and reads it against each estimator.

Read against the empirical column, each estimator's CPR error maps into effective-maturity error: the ABM's over-predicted prepayment implies a book roughly 3.4 years too short at window start, Path A's under-prediction a book 1.5 years too long, and Path B lands within 0.3 years. The table's final row gives the no-shock world, with prepayments continuing at the 22.81\% mean 2021 realized CPR and implying a 3.4-year WAL; against it, the empirical 9.4-year WAL is a 6.0-year extension. That duration gap is what the paper's dollar benchmark prices month by month. The two Danish rows read the counterfactual in the same maturity units. Feeding the Danish legs' mean CPRs through the identical calculator gives a rule-only WAL of 9.1 years at the window's start against the U.S. leg's 9.7---a 0.6-year rule-only shortening, with the Danish-level bracketing case at 10.8. The rule-only leg's extension is additionally rate-invariant on the rate-rise side by the identified moving-flatness (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}). I deliberately price extension, not convexity: option-adjusted duration and convexity require a term-structure and prepayment-option model outside this paper's scope. \citet{hanson2014} shows that the convexity so omitted has aggregate rate-dynamic consequences of its own, which is a reason to flag the omission. The term ``convexity-neutral'' is used strictly in this rate-rise sense.

\section{Fifteen-Year MBS Fold-In and Path B Rate-Gap Units}\label{sec:robustness-pipeline}

Two features of the production pipeline set headline figures cited throughout this paper: how fifteen-year MBS are treated, and the units in which Path B applies its rate-gap coefficient. The replication package's ledger catalogues the figures each supersedes.

The first is the fifteen-year MBS fold-in. The SOMA cohort parser buckets 15-year holdings separately by coupon and never merges 15-year with 30-year buckets, and 15-year scheduled amortization enters both the empirical CPR back-out and the ABM's simulated roll-off. Restricting to 30-year MBS covers 90.6\% of face value; the 15-year book, approximately 9.1\% of holdings, then contributes to total portfolio size but not to cohort-correct amortization or CPR weighting. Folding it in brings coverage to 99.8\% of SOMA face value at a SOMA weighted-average coupon of 2.49\%. Table~\ref{tab:wal}'s note reconciles these coverage components with the audit parse's 90.7\%/9.1\% term split.

I tested, but did not adopt, a fully native treatment in which 15-year loans also draw voluntary CPR from a 15-year-specific surface. It produced out-of-bounds predicted values. The ABM's payment-delta gate---the change in \eqref{eq:annuity}'s monthly payment upon refinancing at the prevailing rate, which drives loss-aversion and mobility-desire responses elsewhere in this paper---breaks down for seasoned 15-year borrowers, whose same-term replacement payment is nearly flat relative to their current payment. Native 15-year surfaces predicted 32\%--61\% CPR against an empirical range of roughly 5\%--8\%, enough to flip the ABM's aggregate trapped-liquidity estimate to a large negative value, far outside the empirically observed range. Production therefore uses a structural-only fold-in: 15-year cohorts contribute real weights and real scheduled amortization, while voluntary CPR for those cohorts is drawn, in the frozen headline specification, from the same-coupon 30-year surface. This is a scope limitation for the production headline. It does not bind the framework: a 15-year-specific behavioral gate---a pure rate-lock penalty for short-amortization loans---is implemented and is the basis of the Berger run's calibration-stage freeze (Table~\ref{tab:danish}, note b). Its adoption for the production headline is deferred to the pre-submission freeze.

The second is the units in which Path B applies its rate-gap coefficient $\beta_1$, calibrated from \citet{liebersohn2024} via the survival-function transform described in Section~\ref{sec:pathb} and expressed as a log-hazard-ratio per 100 bp increase in the rate gap. Applied to the gap in decimal units, the coefficient is numerically inert: at a representative QT-era state (a 3.0\% coupon against a 6.8\% market rate) it yields a multiplier of approximately $\times$1.0026, against the intended suppression of approximately $\times$0.77. Production applies it as $\exp(100\,\beta_1\,\tilde g_{i,t})$, with $\tilde g_{i,t}$ the decimal form of \eqref{eq:pathB}'s rate gap, equivalently $\exp(\beta_1 g_{i,t})$ in \eqref{eq:pathB}'s 100 bp units. The gap is negative when the borrower is locked in, so \eqref{eq:beta1}'s positive $\beta_1$ suppresses prepayment. This application is consistent with Path A's convention of a positive coefficient applied to the gap in comparable units. Production also expresses the Danish counterfactual's rate-gap function in rate-equivalent units, so that the buyback discount properly offsets the locked-in spread. That function is live in the mechanism-substitution variant. The production Danish leg's prepay response, under either Berger-recalibrated anchor of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}, does not consume that function's classification, which is computed all the same, as Section~\ref{sec:pathb} discloses. Section~\ref{sec:pathb} reports the results under this convention; the superseded \$747 billion (97.7\%) microsimulation is catalogued in the replication package's ledger.

Both are structural or mechanical, not changes in modeling philosophy, and both are additive. Disabling either one---30-year-only filtering, or the decimal-unit $\beta_1$ convention---exactly reproduces the corresponding superseded figures catalogued in the replication package's ledger, which I treat as evidence that each is isolated to its intended code paths.

\section{Seasonalizing the Turnover Floor: Floor Dispersion, and Why No Timing Claim Follows}\label{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor}

The three checks above vary the floor's level, its functional form, and its provenance, holding it constant within the window in each case. Two runs in this paper let the floor vary within the window instead: the cyclical variant of Section~\ref{sec:pathb}, which indexes it to the rate path, and the pre-committed seasonal check taken up here, which indexes it to the calendar. Both move the marginal; this check is the one built to isolate why. It asks two questions at once---whether letting the turnover floor vary by calendar month moves the lock-in marginal, and whether the resulting monthly path tracks the empirical benchmark well enough to license a timing claim. The run is \texttt{seasonal\_floor\_timing}; its spec, leg definitions, placebo families, and a frozen timing rule were fixed in the script header before any run executed. The answers are asymmetric, and the second is the more consequential: the marginal does move, but not for the reason the design was built to test, and the timing rule is met without being informative.

The calendar profile is a twelve-cell exposure-weighted annualized turnover CPR measured on deeply out-of-the-money cohort-months. I estimate it on the full cohort-month panel (7{,}756 cohort-months, \$74.2 trillion of exposure, pooled annual CPR 4.187\%), not on the QT window. The window-restricted variant differs in four calendar cells and drives nothing here; I state the basis because an earlier module docstring described the profile as in-window and was wrong. The profile peaks in June, with a peak-to-trough ratio of 2.13. I run four legs, each as a paired central and $\beta_1 = 0$ null: the production constant 4.0\% floor, a constant 4.5\% floor, a \emph{shape-only} leg carrying the calendar profile with its exposure-weighted mean pinned at 4.0\%, and the pre-committed 4.5\%-mean seasonal leg. The shape-only leg is the one that isolates the calendar, because the pre-committed leg confounds shape with the level move the committed sweep already prices. Every leg re-runs the production microsimulation with only \texttt{INVOLUNTARY\_CPR\_ANNUAL} varied by calendar month, and the constant-4.0\% control reproduces the committed production artifacts bitwise (central \$818.530 billion, null \$748.185 billion, marginal $+\$70.345$ billion, $+9.20$ points).

Giving the floor a calendar profile at a held-constant 4.0\% exposure-weighted mean moves the marginal from $+\$70.345$ billion to $+\$65.448$ billion, $+9.20$ to $+8.56$ points: a shape effect of $-\$4.897$ billion. A permutation family disposes of the natural reading of that number. Assign the \emph{same} twelve floor values to scrambled calendar months, so that the multiset of values is preserved exactly and only the month labels change, and 89.2\% of the shape effect survives on average across fourteen draws (69.9\% to 102.8\%); the permuted marginals run \$65.311 to \$66.924 billion against the true \$65.448 billion. Calendar order is very nearly irrelevant to the movement, so the movement is not a calendar effect.

It is instead a Jensen effect operating through the floor's combination rule. Production combines the floor and the voluntary hazard by a hard maximum (\texttt{FLOOR\_MODE = 'max'}, the semantics \eqref{eq:pathB} and the form test above discuss), and at the production floor the floor binds in 36.3\% of evaluated loan-months. Under a maximum, dispersion in the floor is one-sided: where the voluntary hazard already exceeds the floor, the low floor cells are truncated away and contribute nothing, while the high cells bind and lift the effective hazard. Any dispersion in the floor therefore raises book-wide prepayment regardless of which month carries which value---exactly what the permutation family measures. A convexity-matched control separates the small remaining piece. The annual-CPR-to-monthly-hazard transform is itself convex, so the twelve-cell profile is equivalent in level to a flat 4.0090\% floor rather than a flat 4.0000\% one; running that flat leg gives $+\$70.156$ billion, a $-\$0.189$ billion move. Level leakage is thus 3.9\% of the shape effect, and floor dispersion under the maximum rule is the remaining 96.1\%. I report this as a floor-dispersion and functional-form result. It is not evidence that seasonality moves the marginal.

Level and shape do not separate cleanly, and I do not present an additive split as though they did. Measured against the constant-4.0\% control, the level move contributes $-\$12.704$ billion ($-1.66$ points) and the shape move $-\$4.897$ billion ($-0.64$ points); the two together come to $-\$16.040$ billion ($-2.10$ points), leaving an interaction residual of $+\$1.560$ billion ($+0.20$ points). On the simulated monthly paths that residual is larger relative to what it qualifies---mean absolute interaction residual 0.164 points of monthly CPR against mean absolute total movement 0.533 points, so the interaction runs 30.9\% of the total movement. Any level-plus-shape decomposition of this floor must carry the residual; quoting either component alone misstates both.

What the experiment does buy is a narrow bound. Holding the turnover floor flat within the window is a modeling convenience, and this run bounds how much of the constant-floor marginal that convenience was responsible for: at most $\$4.897$ billion of the $+\$70.345$ billion in-sample marginal, 7.0\% of it. That same absolute bound is 11.5\% of the $+\$42.6$ billion headline marginal it is measured against. Of even that, 96.1\% is the maximum rule's response to dispersion. The identified lock-in marginal is not an artifact of a flat floor. That is the whole of what the seasonal design establishes, and it establishes it about levels only.

\paragraph{The timing concession.} The design's second purpose was to test whether a seasonalized floor earns a monthly-timing claim, under a rule frozen before the runs: Theil $U_2$ on first differences below one \emph{and} a positive detrended lag-0 correlation against the empirical benchmark. That rule is met as written by both seasonal legs (shape-only $U_2 = 0.995$, $r = +0.016$; pre-committed leg $U_2 = 0.990$, $r = +0.070$), and I report it as met, because it was frozen ex ante and I will not reopen a rule after seeing which way it fell. I nonetheless claim nothing from the pass, because five diagnostics computed afterwards show the rule cannot discriminate. First, the benchmark carries it. The SOMA back-out has four exact-zero months (June 2022, February 2023, April 2024, September 2025), three of them immediately followed by a spike of up to 14.01\% annualized. The zeros are now traced to their mechanism: each is clip-induced, a month whose reported rolloff falls below the scheduled-amortization floor so that the back-out's non-negativity clip pins it at exactly zero, with the displaced mass posting into the following month's reading (unclipped values $-1.59$/$-0.49$/$-0.84$/$-0.84$ points; run \texttt{h1\_zero\_months\_diagnosis}) --- a month-boundary allocation artifact of the reporting series, not a behavioral observation. The obvious alternative --- rebuilding the series from published monthly principal payments, or from per-CUSIP factor changes --- was probed rather than assumed, and neither route is usable. No monthly SOMA principal-payment series is published: the summary endpoint carries holdings levels only, and both monthly endpoints return HTTP 400. The per-CUSIP route does exist but recovers face levels rather than principal payments --- gross face declines run \$2{,}186 billion against a \$652.8 billion net, since face also falls on reinvestment settlement and roll --- so netted over 192 weekly as-of dates it reproduces the committed month-end differencing, \$652.7517 billion against \$652.7517 billion, and returns the same four low months at $-\$2.0$, \$4.5, \$3.7 and \$3.6 billion against a \$16.8 billion window median. Three of the four sit on a week the New York Fed republished unchanged and June 2022 does not, and republication is ordinary rather than diagnostic --- 59 of those 192 weeks and 36 of the 44 months they span carry one --- so what the rebuild establishes is that the alternative source is unavailable and the zeros survive it, not a second mechanism (run \texttt{benchmark\_monthly\_rebuild}). The June 2022 zero opens the window, is followed by an ordinary month, and so contributes one first difference where the others contribute two. Those pairs touch 7 of 41 first differences in total but carry 0.813 of the empirical differenced signal and 0.796 to 0.811 of every leg's $U_2$ numerator. Repairing them by reallocating each spike evenly across its pair, conserving the pair total, flips both seasonal legs from pass to concede and turns the shape-only leg's detrended correlation from $+0.016$ to $-0.134$. Second, a calendar-rotation placebo moves the true profile to the wrong months while holding its amplitude and dispersion exactly; the rule is cleared by 1 of 11 wrong-month rotations for each seasonal leg, and the five-month rotation \emph{beats} the true profile on $U_2$ (0.9934 against 0.9947). Third, 3 of the 14 scrambled calendar orders above clear the rule, and the best of them attains $U_2 = 0.975$, better than either real seasonal leg. Fourth, and decisively, the $\beta_1 = 0$ null clears the rule on both constant-floor legs, the 4.0\% null doing so with $U_2 = 0.9997$ and a detrended correlation of $+0.115$, stronger than either seasonal central leg's. A criterion satisfied by a specification with the lock-in elasticity removed cannot be evidence for the lock-in mechanism. Fifth, the correlations are not distinguishable from zero on 42 months in any case ($p = 0.921$ and $p = 0.661$), and the leg that isolates the mechanism is the fragile one: the shape-only leg's verdict flips on 5 of 42 single-month deletions while the pre-committed leg's flips on none.

Two further diagnostics point the same way without being needed for the verdict. The seasonal legs concentrate 79.7\% (shape-only) and 89.4\% (pre-committed) of their simulated detrended variance in the twelve-month calendar harmonic; the empirical benchmark carries 0.8\% of its own detrended variance there. The model acquires calendar structure the benchmark can neither confirm nor refute. On the committed cross-correlation metric, meanwhile, the seasonal legs peak at lag $+6$ with a negative correlation, on the boundary of a 42-month window, which I mark non-interpretable.

The right reading of this is a finding about the criterion, not a failure of the experiment. The frozen rule was a reasonable ex-ante test, and it turns out to have no power against the alternatives that matter: a lumpy benchmark, wrong-month profiles, scrambled calendars, and an elasticity-free null all satisfy it. I therefore restrict every claim in this paper to levels. No estimator here earns a monthly-timing claim, which extends to the seasonalized floor the levels-only concession the production microsimulation's monthly path already carries, where the raw lag-0 correlation does not survive detrending (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). This restricts what the design identifies; it retracts no level result, and no aggregate, marginal, or bound reported elsewhere in this paper depends on timing.

\paragraph{Carried caveats.} Four qualifications travel with the seasonal floor wherever it is quoted, and none of them is repaired by the runs above. First, the profile's identification is circular in the same way the in-window floor's is: the deep-out-of-the-money filter that defines it captures 98.2\% of QT exposure, so it is close to the identity map on the panel and the profile is not measured off a lock-in-free population. Second, the clean off-window leg that rescues the floor's \emph{level} cannot rescue its \emph{shape}: the 2017--2019 out-of-the-money cells number 335 cohort-months on \$86.2 billion of exposure and record zero prepaid balance in February and April, so no twelve-cell profile is estimable there at all. Third, the seasonal share is not stable across windows, the calendar terms explaining $R^2 = 0.011$ of the full-panel variation against 0.755 in the QT window, and the profile inherits that instability. Fourth, this floor profile peaks in June while the committed \emph{voluntary} seasonality of the estimated cohort-hazard form peaks in March and October. They are different objects and need not agree, but the tension is real and I state it rather than let the two profiles sit unreconciled in the same paper.

\paragraph{The within-run cyclical floor: dispersion versus co-movement.}
The within-run cyclical floor variant of Section~\ref{sec:pathb}, $\underline{h}_t = 4\% \times (1 + \kappa z_t)$, sweeps $\kappa$ symmetrically about zero. The decomposition of the resulting grid follows.

That range is mostly not a measure of cyclicality, and I no longer read it as one. A separate pre-committed run (\texttt{floor\_cyclical\_permutation}) reproduces all seven committed $\kappa$ cells bit-exactly and then decomposes them. Scrambling the floor path's time order within each $\kappa$ cell, which preserves the multiset of monthly floor values and their mean exactly, leaves 85.0\% of the range span standing: 72 draws, twelve per non-zero $\kappa$, span $+7.20$ to $+9.12$ points against the committed $+7.09$ to $+9.36$. Switching only the combination rule removes it. Under the competing-risks (additive) form the identical $\kappa$ grid spans $+11.22$ to $+11.27$ points, a span of $0.045$ points, so changing the rule alone eliminates 98.0\% of the max-form span; the additive form moves less across the whole $\kappa$ grid than it moves across the 3--5\% constant-floor level sweep. The mechanism is the one-sided Jensen effect that Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor} diagnoses at length for the seasonal floor. Under a hard maximum, dispersion in $\underline{h}_t$ lifts the effective hazard whenever the high cells bind and is truncated away where they do not, regardless of \emph{when} it occurs, so $\kappa$ is a dispersion dial before it is a cyclicality dial. Decomposing the low endpoint confirms it. At $\kappa = +0.5$, which departs $-\$16.09$ billion from production, level equivalence accounts for $-\$2.14$ billion (13.3\%), order-free dispersion for $-\$11.42$ billion (71.0\%), and genuine time-order for $-\$2.53$ billion (15.7\%): 84.3\% of that endpoint is order-free. The high endpoint is not an endpoint at all---it departs from production by $+\$1.23$ billion, $+0.16$ points, at $\kappa = -0.1$.

A cyclicality signal does survive, and it is real but small. The true marginal falls outside the entire twelve-draw permutation range at every one of the six non-zero $\kappa$ (0 of 72 draws bracket it). The order components are monotone in $|\kappa|$ and sign-consistent with the co-movement story the concession in Section~\ref{sec:pathb} states: $+\$5.29$, $+\$4.77$, and $+\$2.69$ billion at $\kappa = -0.5$, $-0.25$, $-0.1$, and $-\$1.42$, $-\$1.75$, and $-\$2.53$ billion at $+0.1$, $+0.25$, $+0.5$. A floor rising with rates does lower the marginal, and one falling with rates does raise it. But scrambling the time order strips most of the grid's asymmetry while leaving its level drop intact: the permuted means differ across the sign of $\kappa$ by only $\$0.22$, $\$1.80$, and $\$6.01$ billion at $|\kappa| = 0.1$, $0.25$, and $0.5$, against a true spread of $\$13.82$ billion at $|\kappa| = 0.5$ ($+\$68.08$ billion at $\kappa = -0.5$ against $+\$54.26$ billion at $+0.5$). A two-sided response that survives losing the time order is the signature of dispersion, not of co-movement. The symmetric part of the grid's movement is large and is dispersion; the asymmetric part is small and is cyclicality. What the grid bounds is therefore the marginal's sensitivity to within-run floor variation of this amplitude, of which at most \$5.29 billion in any cell is attributable to the direction of co-movement. That the concession's residual is bounded by the level sweep already reported holds a fortiori. One correction to the specification as earlier described: the window mean is pinned at the production 4\% only where the floor's non-negativity clip does not bind, and it binds in the two $|\kappa| = 0.5$ stress cells, whose realized window means are 4.0005\% and 4.0828\% (the artifact flags the clip per cell). Of those two, $\kappa = +0.5$ sets the bracket's low end at $+7.09$ points, while $\kappa = -0.5$ sits mid-grid at $+8.90$. The invariant therefore fails in the cell that matters most for the reported range, and the convexity-matched flat floor equivalent to the $\kappa = +0.5$ path is 4.0977\%, not 4\%.
